6.1.3.2 Design Options Analysis 
 
A Design Options Analysis is provided in the Architectural Design Report provided at Appendix 3. The 
analysis illustrates how the proposed site planning and layout is the best methods of achieving good 
design as prescribed by Better Placed as discussed above, and the provisions of the Macquarie Park 
Design Guide. The Proposal went through three major design iterations with a view to addressing the 
Site constraints and opportunities.  
 
The design evolution is illustrated in the following sections: 
 
Initial Concept Design 
 
The initial design proposal for the Proposal primarily organised the facility in a north -south 
configuration, utilising a substantial portion of the Site area. This layout necessitated the removal of a 
significant volume of existing vegetation.  
 
In this design, the Data Halls were concentrated towards the south, with generators facing the street. 
The arrangement also included a Front of House/Administration zone alongside a proposed access 
road to the west, and a substation zone positioned towards Richardson Place on the east.  
 
Figure 23 below illustrates the Initial Concept Design. 
 
 
Figure 23. Initial Concept Design (Source: Greenbox Architecture, 2025) 
 
The initial concept scheme presented several issues: 
 
• Noise Impact: The placement of generators, which typically generate noise, was towards the 
commercial and future residential facilities to the north, potentially causing disturbances. 
• Limited Aesthetic Opportunity: The Generator Gantry/platform offered limited architectural 
design and aesthetic opportunities, detracting from the visual appeal. 

[[page 84]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde 
  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  71  
• Obscured Main Entry: The main entry was located to the southwest down the proposed access 
road and behind a proposed car park, making it less accessible and identifiable. 
• Vegetation Removal: The arrangement required removal of a substantial portion of natural 
vegetation, which was not ideal for environmental sustainability 
• Lack of Connectivity: The proposal did not allow for a site through road to Richardson Place, 
limiting site accessibility and connectivity. 
 
These issues highlighted the need for a revised design that would better balance functional 
requirements with environmental and aesthetic considerations, while also improving accessibility and 
minimising noise impact on surrounding areas 
 
Developed Masterplan 
 
The developed masterplan strategically reoriented the general arrangement of the Data Centre project 
by 90 degrees, resulting in an east -west configuration. Figure 24 below illustrates the Developed 
Masterplan. 
 
 
Figure 24. Development Masterplan (Source: Greenbox Architecture, 2025) 
 
This revised design offers several significant benefits: 
 
• Maximised Vegetation Retention: The new layout maximises the retention of valuable existing 
vegetation and bushland towards the south of the site, enhancing environmental 
sustainability. 
• Strategic Main Entry Location: The main entry and Front of House are strategically positioned 
to ensure they are easily identifiable and accessible. 
• Enhanced Facade Presentation: Positioning the office component to the north results in well -
articulated facades facing the Julius Avenue commercial precinct, contributing to the area’s 
aesthetic appeal. 

[[page 85]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde 
  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  72  
• Street Activation: Having the Front of House component present to Julius Avenue fosters a 
vibrant and engaging streetscape. 
• Noise Minimisation: Generators are located behind the main Data Hall building and positioned 
to the south, minimising sound towards the street and surrounding areas. 
• Improved Connectivity: The arrangement allows for an east-west site through road connecting 
to Richardson Place, enhancing site accessibility. 
 
This thoughtful reconfiguration improves the Data Centre’s integration with the surrounding 
environment, balancing operational efficiency with visual and environmental sensitivity 
 
The Developed Masterplan was further refined to address building massing, façade articulation and 
materiality, access, consistency with the Macquarie Park Design Guide, and operational efficiencies. The 
final design is discussed in detail in Section 6.14 of this EIS.  
 
6.1.4 Built Form and Urban Design  
 
6.1.4.1 Connecting with Country 
 
A Connecting with Country Statement has been included in both the Architectural Design Report and 
Landscape Design Report in accordance with the requirements of the Macquarie Park Design Guide  
and the Macquarie Park Connecting to Country Framework.  
 
The Proposal has been designed  to align with the ‘Connecting to Country’ principles set out in the 
Macquarie Park Design Guide. Recognising the deep cultural significance and enduring custodianship  
of the Traditional Owners of the land, the  Proposal acknowledges the importance of embedding 
Aboriginal perspectives, stories, and relationships to Country into the built environment. 
 
A key element of the design is the incorporation of pedestrian pathways through the  Site, enhancing 
permeability and public access. This feature responds directly to the Guideline’s emphasis on 
improving the pedestrian experience while facilitating deeper physical and symbolic connections with 
the land. The landscaping strategy further supports this connection, with the integration of native plant 
species significant to local Aboriginal communities. This approach creates a sensory and educational 
environment that reinforces a strong connection to Country. 
 
An essential aspect of the Proposal’s sustainability strategy is its response to solar control and passive 
design principles. Informed by Aboriginal knowledge systems that recognise seasonal shifts and the 
role of the sun in understanding and respecting natural rhythms, the project orients it s office and 
administration components to the north. This orientation maximises natural light during winter 
months and allows for shading and solar control in the summer, thereby reducing energy demand. 
 
Importantly, the design incorporates the principle of Designated Keep Sites, acknowledging that some 
parts of Country hold particular significance and should be protected or set aside for low -impact or 
culturally sensitive uses. In the case of the Site, a vast portion of the land is intentionally left 
undeveloped, with existing mature vegetation to be retained. This undisturbed area serves both 
ecological and cultural purposes —preserving habitat, supporting biodiversity, and allowing for 
respectful contemplation and recognition of Country. It represents a tangible commitment to care for 
the land and a refusal to erase or override the natural and cultural narratives embedded within it. 
 
6.1.4.2 Built Form 
 
The Proposal’s massing has been carefully designed to meet operational requirements while 
addressing key Site constraints, including setbacks, pedestrian pathways, and the preservation of 
existing vegetation. The building is generally situated within the required envelope controls, including 
height restrictions, and takes advantage of the Site’s unique topography. 
[[page 86]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde 
  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  73  
 
Positioned primarily in the northern portion of the Site, the building utilises an existing cut to reduce 
its overall height and visual bulk, minimising its impact on surrounding areas, particularly when viewed 
from Julius Avenue. This strategic placement ensures that the building maintains a low profile whi le 
maximising the use of available space. 
 
The main entry and Front of House (FOH) component are oriented to the north to optimise solar access 
for office and administration areas. Service and heavy vehicle access are discreetly located to the south, 
creating a clear separation between operational and visitor functions. Critical power ge nerators are 
positioned to the south of the building to minimise noise impact on nearby commercial and future 
residential areas to the north. This approach ensures that the building’s massing respects the 
surrounding environment and minimises potential disturbances.  
 
This design approach focuses on the optimal placement of the building on the Site, ensuring a 
balanced relationship between functionality, environmental impact, and integration with the 
surrounding context. 
 
The floor-to-floor heights required for a date centre development are precise and based on a required 
volumetric outcome to establish the future internal layouts whilst remaining compliant with BCA 
Standards and fire engineering requirements, e.g. ceiling clearances and fire sprinkler systems. The 
Proposal provides significant floor -to-floor clearances of 6m on all levels. An additional 9 m of rooftop 
plant and ventilation towers are required with acoustic screening, which is required to be located on 
the roof for operational reasons.  
 
The specific volumetric outcomes required for a data centre are guided by the below requirements: 
 
• Whitespace needing to accommodate bulk power and fibre cabling to meet the current cloud 
computing and AI demands; 
• Minimum services and computer server clearance required below ceiling level within the 
whitespace to enable safe access and maintenance works; and 
• High ceiling void needed to act as return air plenum which is essential to maintain efficient 
cooling to critical digital infrastructure. 
 
6.1.4.3 Materials 
 
The choice of materials is both functional and aesthetic, carefully responding to the distinct 
architectural language of Façades A and B. Each material has been selected to support the spatial 
intent while contributing to the overall composition and performance of the building.  
 
The service areas, particularly the stair cores, are constructed from precast concrete with a rugged 
vertical finish. These robust elements ground the building and act as key structural anchors within the 
façade. In contrast, the FOH façade features a ligh ter-toned polished concrete, offering a refined and 
welcoming appearance that complements its role as the public-facing focal point.  
 
For the data halls, the expansive façade is clad in metal panels. This finish not only reflects sunlight to 
create a dynamic play of light and shadow throughout the day, but also visually breaks up the large 
surface area without the use of windows—bringing depth and animation to the otherwise solid façade.  
 
The building materiality is illustrated in Figure 10 below.

6.1.4.4 Building Footprint 
 
The Proposal has a gross floor area (GFA) of 26,076sqm on the 28,630sqm Site. Therefore, the Proposal 
has an FSR which equates to 0.91:1  measured in accordance with the Site area as calculated in 
accordance with Clause 4.5 of RLEP 2014.  
 
It is noted that a base maximum FSR development standard of 1:1 applies to the Site pursuant to Clause 
4.4 of RLEP and an incentive maximum FSR development standard of 1.5:1 applies to the Site pursuant 
to Clause 7.7 of RLEP 2014. The Proposal therefore co mplies with both applicable development 
standards.  
 
The proposed design enables easy movement on the Site, provides parking for employees and EOT 
facilities, and allows for landscaping whilst providing an articulated and visually interesting built form.

6.1.4.5 Building Setbacks 
 
Given the irregular shape of the Site and to provide building articulation, the Proposal provides a 
stepped building form and seeks to provide the following minimum building setbacks: 
 
• 5m to Julius Avenue 
• 5m to south-western side 
• 5m to north-eastern side 
• Substantial setback ranging from 20m to 50m to rear of Site 
 
The Proposal is generally co mpliant with the Macquarie Park Design Guide setback controls with the 
exception of an encroachment into the rear landscaped setback to accommodate the STSS, and a 
minor 1m variation on the front and side setbacks at the narrowest point.  It is noted that the front 
setback increases to 9m and the side setbacks increase to 19m at the widest point of the Site.  
 
The stepped form of the building with varying setbacks is considered to be a more desirable outcome 
than a consistent building setback along the length of the building, given the large floor plates 
proposed. The Proposal is consistent with the following ob jectives of Part 5.2 of the Macquarie Park 
Design Guide: 
 
a) Enhance the character of existing streets and create new streets which contribute to the 
character and identity of the Precinct.  
b) Retain and reinforce the existing character of green setbacks with mature planting. 
c) Ensure views to the sky and views between buildings from the public realm, including 
between buildings on adjoining lots. 
d) Ensure appropriate separation between buildings to protect residential amenity and 
privacy. 
e) Ensure appropriate amenity for the public domain including wind conditions, solar access 
and protection from weather. 
 
The Proposal retains the existing mature planting in the front of the Site and incorporates a minimum 
5m front setback that is almost entirely landscaped to ensure the existing character of green setbacks 
with mature planting is retained.  
 
Given the negligible 700mm maximum variation at the narrowest points of the front setback and 
725mm at the side setbacks, and consistency with the objectives of the control, the Proposal is 
considered to be consistent with the provisions of the Macquarie P ark Design Guide in relation to 
setbacks. Figure 26 below illustrates those parts of the building that encroach into the prescribed 
setback zones in red, and the areas that are compliant in green. The total floor area of the variation on 
the ground floor plane equates to 120sqm which is negligible compared with the total GFA of 
26,076sqm.  
In contrast, significant parts of the ground floor plane are well in exceedance of the setback 
requirement.  
 
It is noted that the STSS encroaches into the rear landscaped setback area prescribed by the Macquarie 
Park Design Guide. The encroachment and extent of necessary vegetation removal is unavoidable 
considering the following Site-specific constraints: 
 
• The footprint of the STSS has been reduced as much as practicable to meet Ausgrid 
specifications,  
• The provision of a new street through the Site to meet the Macquarie Park Design Guide has 
necessitated vegetation removal which is unavoidable to achieve compliance; 
• The width of the proposed new street has been reduced in width from 14m to 10m to reduce 
the footprint of disturbance into the setback; 
[[page 89]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde 
  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  76  
• The extent of tree clearing has been reduced as much as possible by implementing a bush fire 
protection performance solution, as discussed in Section 6.1.22 of this EIS; 
• The provision for the proposed STSS will result in some tree removal, however the BDAR 
identifies that the trees in this location are not of significant biodiversity value and will be 
replaced with compensatory planting on the Site and off-site.  
 
Notwithstanding the encroachment, the Proposal is consistent with the objectives of the setback 
controls as discussed above.

6.1.4.6 Building Height 
 
The Site is subject to a 30m base maximum building height development standard pursuant to Clause 
4.3 of RLEP 2014 and an incentive maximum building height development standard of 45m pursuant 
to Clause 7.7 of RLEP 2014.  
 
The Proposal includes three (3) separate building forms of variable heights, as follows: 
 
• The main data hall building has a height of seven (7) storeys with rooftop plant 
• The office/FOH building has a height of five (5) storeys and wraps around the south -western 
corner of the Site 
• The enclosed generator gantry has a height of six (6) storeys to the rear of the data hall building.  
 
The office/FOH building presents a maximum building height of 38.93m to the Julius Avenue frontage, 
with the taller data hall portion of the building behind and above. 
 

[[page 90]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde 
  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  77  
Given the slope of the Site and existing excavation carried out on the Site, the ground level  of the 
building is substantially below the Julius Avenue footpath level, which significantly reduces the scale 
of the building when viewed from the public domain. The maximum height of the main data hall 
building in the centre of the Site is 50.93m, which constitutes the mesh screening to the rooftop plant 
of the building. Notwithstanding, no part of the substantive building breache s the building height 
standard, only rooftop plant screening. The office/FOH building and enclosed generator gantry 
complies with the building height standard.  
 
The Proposal seeks a maximum building height of 50.93m, which exceeds the 45m incentive 
maximum building height development standard by 5.93m or 13.1%.

A summary of the justifications for the variation are provided below: 
 
• The floor-to-floor heights required for a date centre development are precise and based on a 
required volumetric outcome for each data hall. The Proposal provides significant floor-to-floor 
clearances of 6m on all levels to establish future internal layouts whilst still remaining 
compliant with BCA Standards and fire engineering requirements, e.g. ceiling clearances and 
fire sprinkler systems. The specific volumetric outcomes required for a data centre are guided 
by the below requirements: 
 
o Whitespace needing to accommodate bulk power and fibre cabling to meet the 
current cloud computing and AI demands; 
o Minimum services and computer server clearance required below ceiling level within 
the whitespace to enable safe access and maintenance works; and 
o High ceiling void needed to act as return air plenum which is essential to maintain 
efficient cooling to critical digital infrastructure. 
o High efficiency heat rejection plants have been selected in order to minimised energy 
and scope 2 carbon emissions.   The plants are required to be located 
externally.  Placing them on the roof area further increase efficiency by preventing 
recirculation as  well as minimising risks of bacterial growth contamination to 
occupants. 
 
• The location of plant and equipment on the rooftop level, and surrounded by acoustic 
screening, is the best environmental planning outcome for the Site in that it results in the best 
acoustic mitigation for the plant. The relocation of the rooftop plant to  the ground floor level 
would significantly increase the footprint of the building and reduce any opportunity for 
landscaping and increase the extent of vegetation clearing required on the Site.  
 
[[page 92]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde 
  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  79  
• The Proposal is consistent with the existing and desired future character of the Site, the locality, 
and the surrounding area. The Proposal provides for a scale of development that when viewed 
from the streetscape and surrounding properties is not dominant in terms of bulk, scale, extent 
of hardstand or interface with the streetscape. Further, the Proposal retains significant 
landscaping that promotes the natural environment and includes well designed recreational 
infrastructure. 
 
• The Proposal is consistent with the desired future character of the Site, the locality and the 
surrounding area. The Proposal provides for a scale of development that is consistent with the 
desired future character of the area, which has been shaped by the development standards 
prescribed by the RLEP 2014 and representative of  the implementation of Macquarie Park 
Accelerated TOD Precinct. The Proposal is minimal in scale when compared to 95m MU1 Mixed 
Use zone within 100m of the Site and the height deviation is considered minor and remains 
consistent with the scale of developme nt in the locality (see Woollahra Municipal Council v 
SJD DB2 Pty Ltd [2020 NSWLEC 115]). 
 
• The density (as measured by FSR) is compliant with the 1:1 development standard prescribed 
for the Site by Clause 4.4 of RLEP 2014. The additional height of Proposal is therefore not due 
to a desire to increase FSR. A theoretical scheme that could be reduced in overall height, would 
require a greater footprint and would not be viable due to the existing vegetation within the 
Site. The Proposal represents the best outcome for the Site and the streetscape, providing a 
compliant provision of landscaping that reduces any visual impacts to the adjoining properties 
and acts a suitable transition between the C1 National Parks and Nature Reserves zone and the 
Macquarie Park Accelerated TOD Precinct and the bulk and scale is consistent with the future 
intended development in the locality. 
 
• The Proposal is generally compliant with the minimum building setback requirements 
prescribed by the Macquarie Park Design Guide, providing a minimum 5m setback due to the 
constrained nature of the Site, including the construction of the local road through  the Site 
and the preservation of a landscaped setback to Lane Cove National Park. The setbacks vary 
given the shape of the Site and provide sufficient relief from the side boundaries in accordance 
with the objectives. The Proposal provides a contributory frontage in accordance with the 
Macquarie Park Design Guide, ensuring a commercial active frontage is provided along all 
frontages facing public streets and public open spaces.  
 
• Despite the variation, the Proposal is consistent with the provisions of the Macquarie Park 
Design Guide in the following manner: 
 
o The Shadow Diagrams provided at Appendix 2 to the EIS illustrate compliance with 
the overshadowing controls contained in RDCP 2014 and the Macquarie Park Design 
Guide. There will be some additional shadow cast over the commercial buildings at 10 
Julius Avenue at 9:00am, however, this is reduced as the day progresses, resulting in 
no impact from 12:00pm to 3:00pm. The Proposal is compliant with the solar 
protection requirements of Macquarie Park Design Guide. , ensuring that the 
nominated public open space will have the required amount of solar protection. 
 
o The Proposal includes a window design that reduces the likelihood of bird collision 
risk, with only half of the north elevation of the building proposed to have windows. 
Each window is glazed and non -reflective glass to ensure the chance of any bird 
collision is minimised.  
 
o The Proposal responds to the existing industrial and commercial developments in the 
locality and the height exceedance does not result in any additional or unacceptable 
impact on other land uses by way of visual impact, disruption of views, loss of privacy, 
[[page 93]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde 
  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  80  
loss of solar access, traffic impact or loss of on -street parking capacity of the like . 
Therefore, the Proposal does not result in any unreasonable impacts on adjoining 
development. 
 
o The P roposal focusses the GFA on a constrained footprint to reduce the extent of 
vegetation clearing required on the Site, and retains a significant portion of the 
landscaped setback in accordance with the provisions of the Macquarie Park Design 
Guide. 
 
o The Proposal is also compliant with the landscape setback, tree canopy and deep soil 
targets of The Design Guide, providing 25.16% of the Site area as tree canopy cover and 
32.12% of the Site area as deep soil landscaping. This strengthens tree canopy in t he 
locality and creates green linkages, aiming to ameliorate effects of urban heat island. 
 
o The Proposal allows for the delivery of public infrastructure as prescribed by the 
Macquarie Park Design Guide.  
 
• The Proposal would make efficient and orderly use of a site that is currently considered to be 
under utilised and result in the orderly and economically beneficial development of the land, 
without resulting in any unacceptable economic, environmental or so cial impacts, and is 
consistent with the objectives of the EP&A Act.  
 
• The Proposal is considerate of the adjoining Lane Cove National Park through appropriate 
design, materials, and the rear landscaped setback ensuring the building façade and layout 
minimises impacts to the surrounding area. Through breaching the height limit, the proposal 
is able to retain vegetated setbacks to the National Park which would need to be reduced 
should a compliant scheme be proposed. This facilitates ecologically sustainable development 
through environmental considerations and as a means of protecting the natural environment. 
 
▪ The additional building height beyond the 45m development standard does not cause any 
impact on existing development in the vicinity of the Site by way of visual impact, disruption 
of view, loss of privacy and loss of solar access.  
 
▪ The Proposal has been architecturally designed to provide a materiality and form that sets a 
desirable precedent for future development and ensures that the visual impact of the building 
integrates with the surrounding environment.  
 
For the reasons outlined above, it is considered that the proposed variation to the incentive building 
height development standard under Clause 4.3 is appropriate and can be clearly justified having regard 
to the matters listed within clause 4.6(3)(b) of RLEP 2014.  
 
Accordingly, the abovementioned justifications are considered valid, and in this instance the proposed 
variation is considered to be acceptable. The objectives of the development standard and the zone 
would be upheld as a result of the Proposal. 
 
The proposed development represents a more efficient use of the Site. In light of the above, the 
application of the height of building development standard is therefore unreasonable and unnecessary 
in response to the proposed development.

6.1.4.7 Urban Heat Mitigation 
 
The proposed use of the Site as a data centre inherently produces more heat rejection from the building 
compared to a typical commercial or residential building. However, it is important to note that the 
primary method of heat rejection is via cooling towe rs which work adiabatically. This means that the 
heat is used to vaporise water rather than heat the air.  
 
Additionally, all the “hot” equipment associated with the ventilation and cooling of the data centre is 
located on the roof which is well ventilate d and avoids impacting habitable rooms and communal 
open spaces  on any adjoining sites . The proposed cooling equipment  complies with AS1668.2 
minimum separation to property boundary minimising impacts to neighbouring properties. 
 
Where possible, the Landscape Plan has been designed to retain significant trees on Site. The following 
key actions have been implemented during the design to retain as many significant trees as possible: 
 
• Introduction of performance solution to remove expansive Asset Protection Zone that would 
have otherwise impacted the entirety of the Southern bushland zone. The performance 
solution allowed the APZ to be removed back to the perimeter of the road and STSS along with 
retaining trees to the Julius Avenue frontage and new planting within bushfire canopy limits 
within the APZ. 
• Impacts further reduced by adjusting electrical infrastructure to a 132 -33kv incoming 
arrangement compared with 132-11kv as it reduced the footprint of the STSS. 
• Impacts of external tanks mitigated by creating additional basement level, at significant 
expense, to house all tanks within the building footprint. 
 
The Landscape design of the Julius Avenue Data Centre responds to the growing impacts of the urban 
heat island effect by providing double the existing canopy coverage and the minimising of unshaded 
hard surfaces. Opting for elevated  fiberglass reinforced plastic  (FRP) meshing where appropriate to 
allow for a greater total landscaped area and softer surface finishes as solar buffers to ensure 
appropriate comfort levels for those on-site.  
 
The Proposal’s primary link to the Great North Walk considers principle 2, design action 2 -5 & 9 of the 
Greener Places Framework by better connecting the local community to interlinked green spaces and 
providing both public and ecological amenity to Site whilst minimising the fragmentation of core 
bushland. Promoting stewardship and connection with natural processes in alignment with principle 
3’s multifunctionality 
 
Given the above, and the significant vegetation being retained and reinstated on the Site, the Proposal 
is not considered to contribute significantly to the urban heat island effect.  
 
6.1.4.8 Building Frontage 
 
The Site is identified on the Macquarie Park Design Guide Contributory Frontages Map and therefore is 
required to provide an active frontage along Julius Avenue.  
 
Part 5.4 of the Macquarie Park Design Guide prescribes detailed controls for  “office premises, business 
premises, and retail premises” , however, they do not envision other permi ssible uses, such as data 
centres which may not be able to provide a continuous active frontage.  
 
 
The Proposal provides a FOH/office frontage along approximately half of the Julius Avenue frontage, 
with the remainder being provided as a landscape setback area with the retention of mature trees and 
additional landscaping, which is consistent with the setb ack provisions of the Macquarie Park Design 
Guide. 
[[page 95]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde 
  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  82  
 
The building facade to Julius Avenue is consistent with the objectives of Part 5.4 in that the Proposal 
contributes to pedestrian safety by improving the public domain and removing the redundant 
vehicular crossing from Julius Av enue and contributes to the overall commercial character of the 
precinct. 
 
Given the high-level security requirements of the Site and interface with the public domain. the fencing 
along the north -western boundary of the Site is 1500mm high and constructed from a simple wire 
mesh. This design choice was influenced by the Council’s preference to maintain the commercial 
streetscape along Julius Avenue, avoiding the visual obstruction that more substantial fencing might 
cause. The wire mesh fencing was selected as an appropriate response to this concern, offering a high 
degree of transparency and minimal visual impact while still providing a secure delineation of the Site. 
The fence will be substantially screened by proposed vegetation and is appropriate in its context.

the NCC 2022 Part 4D, F4, and E3 and Disability (Access to premises Buildings Standards 2010 and 
pertaining standards and will meet the intent of the Disability Discrimination Act 1992 and NSW Anti -
Discrimination Act 1977.

Appendix 5 of this EIS. The BCA Assessment Report considers the design of the Proposal against the 
Deemed-to-Satisfy Provisions of the BCA 2022 and demonstrates the proposal is capable of 
compliance with the BCA subject to future design development to be undertaken at CC stage.  The 
BCA Assessment Report prescribes that a Section J Report will be required to be provided at CC stage.  
 
6.1.5 Visual Impact 
 
A Visual Impact Assessment (VIA) has been prepared by Geoscapes in support of the Proposal and is 
provided at Appendix 7. The purpose of the VIA is as follows: 
 
• To identify existing environmental values surrounding the Site and assess key viewpoints. 
• Verify publicly accessible and representative viewpoints with photographic recording so as to 
provide a representation of  possible and typical views from each locality relevant to the  
Proposal. These viewing  situations reflect particular landscape  and/or visual features of 
importance within the environment and local landscape character. 
• Represent views from key visual receptors (public and private  receivers) where potential 
significant change in view might be realised. 
• Review existing information and collate all relevant background  information including 
planning, land use and regional landscape characteristics.

will be on those receptors in the immediate vicinity. 
 
6.1.5.2 Site Visit and Analysis of Zone of Visibility 
 
Site visits were conducted during March and April of 2025 by Geoscapes. The team carried out a site 
inspection to verify the results of a desktop study and to evaluate the existing visual character of the 
area. Analysis from inside of the Site boundary was undertaken to approximate the Zone of Visibility.  
 
Photographs taken at eye level from the site would be limiting and not provide a judgement on which 
properties/locations in the immediate vicinity may see the development from ground level to the top 
of the data centre building. This is due to the presence  of other existing buildings and vegetation on 
and surrounding the Site and therefore, it is not possible to gain a complete understanding of the 
development zone of visibility. Instead drone photography was utilised.

6.1.5.3 Assessment of Impacts 
 
The VIA considers that the Proposal will create minor to high visual impacts from the twenty-three (23) 
viewpoints. The assessment confirms the moderate to high /moderate visual impacts are generally 
limited to those viewpoints within proximity to the Site, apart from VP3 which is approximately 500m 
to the south-east, however, has a direct sightline toward the proposed development.  
 
From longer distance views, such as those experienced in residential areas of North Ryde and Lane Cove 
North, the Proposal would only result in a small change in view when compared to a visual baseline. 
 
The visual impacts received by those in very close proximity to the Site vary from minor to high , with 
existing buildings and vegetation influencing the visibility of the proposed development. 
 
Although the building massing and associated infrastructure will be clearly noticeable from much of 
the immediate surrounds, the sensitivity of this location is considered to be medium due to the 
surrounding Corporate Park context and the fact that the type of people most likely to experience the 
view will be workers from adjacent buildings.  
 
The visibility of the Proposal will vary from locations along Lane Cover River such as VP6 and VP23. 
Walking tracks within Great North Walk and Lane Cove Valley Walk are set back from the shoreline and 
therefore, generally the development is screened by vegetation. There are locations when reaching the 
water’s edge is possible and views would be similar to those seen in VP6. This view is also 
representational of those that would be experienced by recreational boats or pleasure marine craft. 
 
The conclusions of potential visual impacts have been determined by site visits, desktop study, 
photographic and photomontage visual analysis. 
 
The VIA notes the  design team have considered finishes on the building facades to help reduce the 
visual impacts of the Proposal. The building utilises texture, colour and high -quality materials upon 
building facades.  
 
A high quality unique architectural design has been applied to the northern facade facing Julius 
Avenue and the public domain. Likewise, interest is provided upon the southern facade which faces 
Lane Cove National Park and some residential areas. These have been included to provide articulation 
and visual interest to help mitigate the appearance of bulk and scale and allow the development to 
present more like a high-end commercial office rather than a typical data centre.  
 
Through analysis conducted within the VIA, of the receptors assessed, the following five (5) locations 
are judged to receive minor visual impacts from the Proposal: 
 
• Rotary Athletics Field, Lane Cove North (VP5) 
• Level 3 – City of Ryde Council Offices (VP8) 
• Childcare Centre, 5A Julius Ave, North Ryde (VP14) 
• Ryde Garden Apartments, North Ryde (VP19) 
• Driveway of No.18 Gilda Street, North Ryde (VP21) 
The following six (6) locations are judged to receive moderate/minor visual impacts from the proposed 
development: 
• Epping Road, Lane Cove West (VP1) 
• Magaret Place, Lane Cove North (VP2) 
• Close to junction of Epping Road & Mowbray Road, Lane Cove North (VP4) 
• Julius Ave (on approach from west), North Ryde (VP16) 
• Close to No.30 Warwick Street, North Ryde (VP20) 
• Lane Cove Valley Walk, Lane Cove North (VP23)

6.1.5.4 Conclusions 
 
The design team have considered finishes on the building facades to help reduce the visual impacts of 
the Proposal. The building utilises texture, colour and high-quality materials upon building facades. In 
particular a high quality unique architectural de sign has been applied to the northern facade facing 
Julius Avenue and the public domain. Similar interest is provided upon the southern facade which 
faces Lane Cove National Park and some residential areas. These have been included to provide 
articulation and visual interest to help mitigate the appearance of bulk and scale. From both the north 
and south it can be argued that visually the development does not look like a ‘typical data centre’ and 
has more treatments that are associated with commercial high-end developments.  
 
As the Proposal is assessed on a merit basis in isolation, the change in view has often been assessed as 
high to very high when considering the existing baseline and the proximity to visual receivers. However, 
the provisions within the RLEP 2014 allow a maximum building height of 30m, a maximum incentive 
building height of  45m and the Proposal is generally compliant with these development standards, 
with the exception of a building height variation in the south-eastern corner of the development where 
the ro oftop plant screening exceeds the height standard by 5.93m.  The footprint of the building is 
significantly constrained by large portions of the Site undevelopable due to dense bushland , 
infrastructure requirements, and steep topography, and therefore the building has been increased in 
height to reduce the ground floor plane.  
 
Given the applicable building height development standards, any building that achieves the maximum 
building height for the Site would still create a high magnitude of change for visual receptors in close 
proximity to the Site such as the Oracle building to the north and some loss of views. It should also be 
noted that the proposed facade facing Julius Avenue is well below the  incentive building  height 
development standard and the rear generator gantry is also below the building height standard. The 
Proposal results in only a minor variation to the incentive building height development standard at the 
rear portion of the building for rooftop plant, with no  part of the  substantive building exceeding the 
height limit.  
 
On this basis, the Proposal is considered to be acceptable from a visual impact perspective.  
 
6.1.6 Traffic, Transport and Accessibility 
 
A Traffic and Parking Impact Assessment (TIA) has been prepared and is provided at Appendix 8 to 
address the following matters required by the SEARs: 
 
• Provide a transport and accessibility impact assessment, which includes:   
o details of all traffic types and volumes likely to be generated during construction and 
operation, including a description of key access and haul routes.   
o an assessment of the predicted impacts of this traffic on road safety and the capacity 
of the road network, including consideration of cumulative traffic impacts on existing 
performance levels of nearby intersections (using industry standard modelling).    
o plans demonstrating how all vehicles likely to be generated during construction and 
operation and awaiting loading, unloading or servicing can be accommodated on the 
Site to avoid queuing in the street network.   
o details and plans of any proposed internal road network, loading dock provision and 
servicing, on-site parking provisions, and sufficient pedestrian and cyclist facilities, in 
accordance with the relevant Australian Standards.  
o swept path analysis for the largest vehicle requiring access to the development.  
o details of road upgrades, infrastructure works, or new roads or access points required 
for the development if necessary.

Assessment Criteria 
 
The SIDRA Intersection 9.1 modelling software was used to assess the peak -hour operating 
performance of the BAESA Williamtown development precinct entry road network. The criteria for 
evaluating the operational performance of intersections are provided by the Guide to Traffic Generating 
Development (TfNSW, 2022). The following performance measures have been reported for each 
scenario to quantify intersection performance. 
 
• Degree of Saturation – The DOS is a measure of the demand for movement in comparison to 
the total capacity. DOS above 1.0, for example, represents oversaturated conditions where 
demand flows exceed capacity, and the degree of saturation below 1.0 represents under -
saturations whe re demand flows are below capacity. Table 18 lists the practical degrees of 
saturation for different intersection types. If the value is greater than the corresponding values 
provided in the table for any movement, then the intersection requires appropriate treatment 
to maintain an acceptable level of DOS.  
• Average Delay —The average delay encountered by all vehicles passing through the 
intersection. It is often important to review the average delay of each approach, as a side road 
could have a long delay time, while the large, free -flowing major traffic will provide an ov erall 
low average delay. 
• 95% Queue Lengths (m) – Is defined as the queue length in metres that has only a 5 -percent 
probability of being exceeded during the analysis period. It transforms the average delay into 
measurable distance units. 
• Level of Service (LOS) – This is a categorisation of average delay intended for simple reference. 
It is a good indicator of overall performance for individual intersections. The operational 
performance assessment of intersections is based on "Level of Service" (LOS), a quantitat ive 
measure related to the average delay experienced by vehicles. Typically, a LOS D or better is 
considered to be an acceptable level of operation.

ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT

Results 
 
The intersection of Delhi Road/Julius Avenue/ Plassey Road and the Intersection of Julius Avenue/ 
Richardson Place is expected to operate at an acceptable Level of Service (LOS) D or better.  
 
However, SIDRA results indicate that the Delhi Road/Julius Avenue/Car Park Entry/Exit intersection is 
projected to operate at LOS F during the PM peak hour in 2036 without development trips, with an 
average delay of 92 seconds and a degree of saturation (DoS) of 1.1, indicating it is slightly over capacity.  
 
When development traffic is introduced, the intersection remains at LOS F, with only a marginal 
increase in delay (95 seconds) and no change in DoS (1.1). However, the 95th percentile queue length 
increases by 32 metres (equivalent to 5 vehicles), suggesti ng that the additional traffic has a minimal 
impact on the surrounding road network. It is worth noting that the 95th percentile queue only occurs 
for 3 minutes of the entire 60-minute period. 
 
The Delhi Road/Julius Avenue Car Park Entry/Exit intersection shows marginally better performance in 
the Future with Development scenario compared to the 2036 Base Case. This improvement results 
from SIDRA's allocation of 1 second less to the Eastbound through movement due to signal 
optimisation, which r educes traffic volume through Delhi Road/Julius Avenue/Plassey Road and 
decreases eastbound delay at the car park entry/exit by 1 second. 
[[page 137]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  124  
6.1.6.3 Parking Provision 
 
Car Parking 
 
Part 9.3 of RDCP 201 4 prescribes a maximum car parking rate specifically for new industrial 
development (which includes data centres as a type of light industry) for t he Site located within the 
Macquarie Park Corridor. The provision rate is a maximum of 1 space per 60sqm. Given the total GFA of 
26,076sqm, this equates to a maximum of 436 spaces.  
 
The Proposal seeks to provide 54 car parking spaces within the basement, including two (2) accessible 
car parking spaces. Therefore, the Proposal is compliant with the provisions of RDCP 2014.   
 
Applying a standard parking ratio would not accurately reflect the unique operational demands of a 
data centre. The Proposal provides 5 4 car parking spaces which aligns with its operational needs. 
However, to accommodate potential future commercial tenants and visitors, providing a few additional 
parking spaces would enhance tenant and customer accessibility, improving the project’s overa ll 
appeal and commercial viability  
 
Bicycle Parking 
 
The Proposal provides six (6) bicycle parking spaces in the basement in accordance with the provisions 
of RDCP 2014.  The bicycle parking is co -located with end of trip facilities in the basement , which 
includes two (2) male and two (2) female showers and lockers, and an accessible shower.  
 
6.1.6.4 Vehicular Access and Design  
 
Car Access 
 
The passenger vehicle driveway is accessible from the shared access road on the western side of the 
Site which is also used by No. 10 Julius Avenue. An additional access point is provided from Richardson 
Place cul-de-sac, forming a new two-way connection to the basement car park.  
 
The new street connection with Richardson Place is being delivered as part of a wider strategy to create 
a new access network as identified within the Macquarie Park Design Guide.  
 
Following extensive consultation with  Council, the new street is proposed to remain in private 
ownership and not be dedicated to Council as prescribed by the Macquarie Park Design Guide.  
 
Service Vehicle Access 
 
Service vehicles will use the same access roads as passenger cars, entering either via Julius Avenue or 
Richardson Place. Due to width restrictions, the swept path analysis is provided in Appendix 3 of the 
TIA. According to the swept path analysis, MRVs will enter and exit the service yard and loading docks 
in a forward direction.  
 
Servicing and Deliveries  
 
Dock provision in the proposed development is made for two MRVs. See Appendix 3 of the TIA for the 
swept path assessment of the access and egress. Waste collection is expected to occur one to two times 
weekly, as per typical waste collection schedule. The Site loading dock will be managed to exclude 
service vehicle access when the regular waste collection is programmed, and a Loading Dock 
Management Plan can be developed should this be required. 
 
  
[[page 138]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  125  
6.1.6.5 Green Travel Plan 
 
A GTP has been prepared by PTC and is provided at Appendix 10. A GTP is a package of measures to 
promote and encourage sustainable travel and reduce reliance on private cars. The overall intention of 
the GTP is to encourage and facilitate the use of alternative and sustainable modes of transport and to 
reduce single -occupancy car travel for journeys to and from the Site. The primary objectives of the 
future GTP will be to: 
 
• Reduce the environmental footprint of the site. 
• Set future staff travel mode share targets. 
• Improve access, amenity, convenience, and safety of sustainable transport modes to/from the 
Site. 
• Promote the use of ‘active’ transport modes such as walking and cycling, particularly for short-
medium distance journeys. 
• Reduce reliance on the use of private vehicles for all journeys. 
• Encourage a healthier, happier, and more active & public transport use culture. 
 
The key objectives for travel to the Site, which the GTP aims to encourage, include: 
 
• Facilitating, encouraging and promoting the use of public and active transport, 
• Promoting the uptake and continued increase in carpool transport, 
• Reduce dependence on private transport, or when required, encourage more efficient use.  
 
There are a number of actions identified in the GTP which can be employed to encourage non -car 
modes of transport to and from the Site. The GTP outline s potential strategies that can be adopted in 
achieving future transport targets. 
 
6.1.6.6 Construction Traffic  
 
A Preliminary CTMP has been prepared by PTC and is provided at Appendix 9. The Preliminary CTMP 
aims to ensure the safety of all workers and road users within the vicinity of the construction site and 
provides a series of strategies to address key construction impacts on traffic, pedestrian safety, and local 
transport services. The CTMP identifies appropriate vehicle routes, outlines site access strategies, and 
suggests control measures to minimise dis ruptions both internal within the site and external on the 
wider road network and environment. Once a Contractor has been appointed, a detailed Construction 
Traffic Management Plan will be prepared. 
 
6.1.6.7 Mitigation Measures  
 
In summary, the traffic and parking implications are not expected to result in any discernible adverse 
impact on the surrounding road network, with management measures to ensure minimal traffic and 
parking implications for both the construction and operation of the proposed development. 
 
The Preliminary CTMP should be further developed, and a detailed CTMP that complies with the 
relevant standards and guidelines will be submitted prior to the commencement of construction 
works.

6.1.7.1 Tree removal 
 
An Arboricultural Impact Assessment (AIA) has been prepared in support of the Proposal and is 
provided as an appendix to the Landscape Plan provided at Appendix 11. A total of 226 trees and tree 
groups were a ssessed within the AIA. The trees comprise of a mix of locally indigenous, Australian -
native and exotic species. 23 species are represented across the Site. A summary of trees impacted 
directly by way of encroachment into the Tree Protection Zone (TPZ) by the proposed construction 
footprint is outlined in the AIA.  
 
A summary of the tree removal and retention is outlined below:   
 
• Retain: A total of 38 trees are  proposed for retention  (not including trees within the dense 
bushland area) 
• Remove: A total of 509 trees (in 188 tree groups) are proposed for removal 
 
It is further noted that a significant number of the trees proposed for removal (approximately 200 trees) 
are low retention value and are likely to be regrowth since the Site was excavated as part of previous 
works on the Site.

6.1.7.2 Tree replacement 
 
The Macquarie Park Design Guide does not prescribe a tree planting ratio for compensatory planting 
but prescribes no net loss of tree canopy and canopy and deep soil targets of 25% and 20% of the Site 
area, respectively. The Proposal complies with the canopy and deep soil targets of the Macquarie Park 
Design Guide. 
 
A Landscape Plan is provided at Appendix 11 that demonstrates the planting of a total of 143 new trees 
on the Site to compensate for loss of tree canopy. Where there is still a net loss of canopy, the Proposal 
will supplement this via off-site planting at the applicable contribution rate per tree  as a monetary 
contribution to Council. It is further noted that the Site is bush fire prone land, and therefore the extent 
of any compensatory planting is limited to comply with the provisions of PBP.  
 
It is understood that amendments to the tree provisions contained within Part 9.5 of RDCP 2014 have 
undergone public exhibition. The draft DCP prescribes a replacement tree planting rate of 3:1, however 
this is  inconsistent with the Macquarie Park Design Guide , and the controls of the Macquarie Park 
Design Guide prevails to the extent of any inconsistency.   
 
Furthermore, approximately 200 trees are located within the previously excavated area on the Site. This 
area was previously cleared and excavated as part of Determination No. 1395/1999 and have regrown 
over the past 15 years. Council has advised that they will consider the exempt from the tree 
replacement contribution payable to Council.

▪ The Forecourt to Julius Avenue which includes public domain upgrades with new grass verge 
and concrete footpath. A new shared forecourt is provided with new tree planting, garden 
rooms, and with sandstone block seating; 
▪ Two (2) new pedestrian through -site links along the north -eastern and south -western 
boundaries of the Site with cascading roof planting; 
▪ An elevated boardwalk through the south -western bushland on the Site, resting and viewing 
decks, and connection points to existing bush walking tracks; 
▪ Street tree planting along the south-western driveway; and

Richardson place.

area; 
• Various bushland regeneration areas; 
• 7,203sqm or 25.16% of the Site area as canopy cover 
• 9,196sqm or 32.12% of the Site area as deep soil landscaping.

A Landscape Design Report has also been prepared in Support of the Proposal and is provided at 
Appendix 12 . Landscaping across the Site  has been designed to meet the objectives for design 
excellence as in accordance with relevant planning provisions and good design as per the Government 
Architect NSW 2017, ‘Better Placed’ document. This has been achieved in the following ways: 
 
Better Fit 
 
The design of public spaces and the new pedestrian network aligns with the Macquarie Park Design 
Guide and supports the evolving needs of the broader neighbourhood beyond the development 
boundary. It embraces the unique character of the Sydney Coastal Enri ched Dry Sandstone Forest by 
incorporating plant species from local vegetation communities, ensuring healthy growth and 
enhancing the adjacent bushland ecosystem 
 
Better Performance 
 
A diverse mix of native planting is used throughout the Site as a key ecological strategy. This approach 
reduces reliance on irrigation and enhances biodiversity. Existing trees are retained where possible, and 
new canopy trees are proposed along Julius Avenue and the new pedestrian connections. These green 
elements improve pedestrian comfort, provide shade, reduce urban heat, and strengthen green links 
 
Better for Community 
 
New publicly accessible open spaces are integrated throughout the Proposal to create a welcoming 
and shared environment for both staff and residents. The public realm extends into the southern 
bushland via accessible boardwalks, designed with gentle gradients and frequent rest points.  
Opportunities for Indigenous artworks are incorporated to acknowledge and strengthen the site’s 
Connecting with Country values. 
 
Better for People 
 
The design supports a healthy and connected community by establishing new pedestrian routes and 
boardwalks that link the future neighbourhood to the Great North Walk and Lane Cove National Park. 
These boardwalks feature viewing platforms and rest areas that encourage community interaction with 
nature and provide moments of reflection and respite 
 
Better Working 
 
Functionality is a key driver of the  Proposal’s landscape design, ensuring pathways and public spaces 
are usable and provide clear routes for pedestrians.  
 
Better Value 
 
The landscape design incorporates durable and easy to maintain materials, reducing operational costs 
and environmental impacts. Low water -use and native planting ensures long -term viability and 
reduces maintenance.   
 
Better Look and Feel 
 
Greenery and open space are prioritised as a respectful response to the adjacent Lane Cove National 
Park, offering a nature -oriented aesthetic that softens the visual impact of the data centre 
development. Material selection adheres to the Ryde Public Doma in Guidelines, ensuring consistency 
across the precinct. Key meeting points  are emphasised with warmer, site -responsive materials to 
enhance the user experience and sense of place.

6.1.8 Ecologically Sustainable Development (ESD)  
 
An Ecologically Sustainable Development (ESD) Report has been prepared in support of the Proposal 
and is provided at Appendix 13 . The ESD Report details the various sustainability measures and 
environmentally conscious initiatives, including principles, project commitments incorporated in the 
design of the Proposal. Additionally, the ESD Report includes a Greenhouse Gas (GHG) Emissions and 
Mitigation Plan. 
 
Addressing the applicable sustainability objectives of the sus tainable Buildings SEPP and Section 193 
of the EP&A Regulation 2021, the ESD Report incorporates strategies to achieve optimised energy 
generation, reduced greenhouse gas emissions and potable water use, optimised resource use and 
minimisation of waste throughout the whole lifec ycle of the building.  These measures directly 
demonstrate how the development will meet or exceed the relevant industry recognised building 
sustainability and environmental performance standard. 
 
The ESD Report addresses the following matters as required by the SEARs for the Proposal: 
 
• Identify how ESD principles (as defined in section 193 of the EP&A Regulation) are 
incorporated in the design and ongoing operation of the development.  
• Demonstrate how the development will meet or exceed the relevant industry recognised 
building sustainability and environmental performance standards.  
• Demonstrate how the development minimises greenhouse gas emissions (reflecting the 
Government’s goal of net zero emissions by 2050) and consumption of energy, water 
(including water sensitive urban design) and material resources.  
• If Chapter 3 of the Sustainable Buildings SEPP applies: 
o Demonstrate how the development has been designed to address the provisions set out 
in Chapter 3.2(i) 
o Provide a NABERS Embodied Emissions Material Form to disclose the amount of 
embodied emissions attributable to the development in accordance with Section 35BA 
of the EP&A Regulation 
 
6.1.8.1 ESD Principles 
 
The precautionary principle 
 
The precautionary principle is that if there are threats of serious or irreversible environmental damage, 
lack of full scientific certainty should not be used as a reason for postponing measures to prevent 
environmental degradation. In applying the precaut ionary principle, public and private decisions 
should be guided by—  
 
• careful evaluation to avoid, wherever practicable, serious or irreversible damage to the 
environment, and   
• an assessment of the risk-weighted consequences of various options. 
 
The Proposal has the potential to consume significant amounts of energy and water. Accordingly, a 
range of measures have been considered to reduce the environmental impact of the proposal and 
ensure compliance with this principle.  
 
Energy consumption will be minimised through optimisation of mechanical systems including higher 
operating temperature set points, an energy management system and high efficiency equipment. The 
Proposal will be designed to achieve a 5-star NABERS Energy for Data Centre Infrastructure rating. 
 
  
[[page 144]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  131  
Water consumption will be minimised through a range of measures including multi-layered metering 
strategies, rainwater harvesting, and deploying high -efficiency equipment to achieve a balance 
between energy consumption and water consumption. In addition, a  specific water consumption 
target for the proposed development has been set to drive the water efficiency of the design. 
 
Furthermore, the Proposal is supported by numerous specialist studies which assess the impact on 
different aspects of the environment, including fauna, flora, soil, and other environmental factors, as 
well as demonstrate systematic and methodical approach to environmentally friendly construction 
that answers to site specific environmental risks. The specialist reports considered throughout this EIS 
collectively ensure the Proposal does not incur adverse impacts to the environment, alleviating the 
concern of environmental damage to align with this principle. 
 
Inter-generational equity 
 
The principle of inter-generational equity is that the present generation should ensure the health, 
diversity and productivity of the environment are maintained or enhanced for the benefit of future 
generations. 
 
Data centres are critical infrastructure, that not only provide employment -generating opportunities 
within the Information, Communication and Technology (ICT) sector, but also support the operation of 
business to business and business to consumer services.  The storage of cloud and electronic data is a 
fundamental for both business and consumer transactions. By supporting productivity and helping to 
drive business activity that underpins current and future economic growth, the proposed development 
will positively impact future generations.  
 
In addition, the proposed development will seek to minimise greenhouse gas emissions through 
operational energy efficiency measures and on -site renewables. Reducing the upfront embodied 
carbon footprint of the proposed development will also minimise the potential detrimental impact to 
future generations. 
 
Other relevant initiatives include reducing waste to landfill through construction and demolition as 
well as operationally, which is covered in the Waste and Recycling Management Plan submitted 
separate to this report. Climate change resilience and Connect ion with Country design principles are 
embedded in responses to the Macquarie Park Design Guide. 
 
Conservation of biological diversity and ecological integrity 
 
The principle of the conservation of biological diversity and ecological integrity is that the conservation 
of biological diversity and ecological integrity should be a fundamental consideration. 
 
The Proposal has been designed to avoid and minimise impacts on biodiversity values in keeping with 
the purposeful use of the Site and its position adjacent to the Lane Cove River and adjoining bushland. 
 
The BDAR and Landscape Plans prepared for the Proposal fundamentally facilitates the conservation 
of biological diversity and ecological integrity by ensuring local flora and dependent fauna are 
supported.  
 
This is achieved through bushland regeneration in the southern portion of the site, the retention of 38 
existing trees and the planting of 143 new trees outside of the bushland. Overall, 7,203m2 or 25% of the 
Site area as will have tree canopy cover, while 9,196m 2 or 32% of the Site will be used for deep soil 
landscaping. 
 
The substantial amount of green infrastructure within the Site will contribute to mitigating the urban 
heat island effect, provide habitat for native fauna and improve the quality of stormwater runoff. 
[[page 145]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  132  
 
While the existing deciduous street trees along the Julius Avenue frontage are not native, the Proposal 
has been designed to avoid impacting these trees as they have well established canopies which assist 
mitigate the urban heat island effect. 
 
Improved valuation, pricing and incentive mechanisms 
 
The principle of improved valuation, pricing and incentive mechanisms is that environmental factors  
should be included in the valuation of assets and services, such as—  
 
• polluter pays, that is, those who generate pollution and waste should bear the cost of 
containment, avoidance, or abatement, and   
• the users of goods and services should pay prices based on the full life cycle of the costs of 
providing the goods and services, including the use of natural resources and assets and the 
ultimate disposal of waste, and   
• established environmental goals should be pursued in the most cost -effective way by 
establishing incentive structures, including market mechanisms, that enable those best placed 
to maximise benefits or minimise costs to develop their own solutions and resp onses to 
environmental problems. 
 
Tenant electricity consumption will be the main driver of carbon emissions for the facility. Electricity 
consumption from the tenant activities at the facility will be under the direct operational and financial 
control of the tenant. The proponent will sup port tenants who choose to self -procure renewables to 
offset their emissions in the facility. 
 
The proponent will work with utility providers, energy retailers and renewable project developers to 
offer the data centre tenants innovative and commercially attractive renewable electricity supply 
options. At the same time, the proponent will support tenants who choose to self-procure renewables 
to offset their Scope 2 emissions in the facility. The proponent will support this outcome by providing 
granular breakdowns of energy consumption and associated emissions. 
 
6.1.8.2 State Environmental Planning Policy (Sustainable Buildings) 2022 
 
All new non -residential developments must report on embodied emissions, where the NSW 
government's Sustainable Buildings SEPP applies. Chapter 3 – Standards for non -residential 
development of the Sustainable Buildings SEPP applies to the Proposal.  Clause 3.2 - Development 
consent for non-residential development of the SEPP prescribes the following: 
 
(1) In deciding whether to grant development consent to non -residential development, the 
consent authority must consider whether the development is designed to enable the 
following— 
(a) the minimisation of waste from associated demolition and construction, including by 
the choice and reuse of building materials, 
(b) a reduction in peak demand for electricity, including through the use of energy efficient 
technology, 
(c) a reduction in the reliance on artificial lighting and mechanical heating and cooling 
through passive design, 
(d) the generation and storage of renewable energy, 
(e) the metering and monitoring of energy consumption, 
(f) the minimisation of the consumption of potable water. 
 
(2) Development consent must not be granted to non -residential development unless the 
consent authority is satisfied the embodied emissions attributable to the development 
have been quantified.

6.1.9 Biodiversity  
 
Clause 7.9 of the NSW Biodiversity Conservation Act 2016 (the BC Act) prescribes that all SSD 
applications for development consent must be accompanied by a BDAR. It is noted that a BDAR waiver 
has not been granted.

provides a summary of the findings of the BDAR.  
 
Biodiversity Values 
 
Two native plant community types were recorded on the Site, including the dominant Sydney Coastal 
Enriched Sandstone Forest (PCT 3592) totalling 1.78 hectares and Northern Lower Floodplain Eleocharis 
Wetland (PCT 3967) totalling 0.13 hectares. The northern portion of the Site has been heavily disturbed 
due to excavation of sandstone bedrock in 2009 and contains only regrowth vegetation. The southern 
portion of the Site contains mostly native forest vegetation in moderate to good condition.

The remaining parts of the Site contain hardstands in the form of exposed sandstone bedrock and

6.1.10 Back-up Power System 
 
This section of the EIS provides further details relating to the proposed back -up power system, as 
requested by the SEARs, addressing the following specific matters: 
 
▪ Provide a detailed overview of any proposed back-up power system, including the scale and 
capacity of the system, and any associated testing procedures (frequency and duration). 
▪ Provide a detailed justification for the proposed back -up power system, including 
alternatives considered. 
 
In order to operate successfully the Proposal is required to demonstrate it can provide sufficient 
operational resilience such that in the event of a failure, crisis or catastrophic event the data centres 
can continue to operate and maintain normal business operation in accordance with agreed business 
arrangements.  
 
6.1.10.1 Requirement for back-up power supply 
 
The Proposal is proposed to be supplied by a new Ausgrid 132kV/33kV STSS. The power supply will be 
distributed within the Site via the Proponent’s own underground electrical infrastructure. The new 
STSS will supply the proposed data centre.  
 
The data centre is a mission critical facility and therefore requires back up generation if there is 
interruption to the utility supply. The Proposal will be provided with diesel -generators and 
uninterrupted power supply (UPS) systems. The UPS system is designed to maintain supply between 
loss of utility and changeover to the generator supply ; it is not intended to maintain the full load on 
Site for an extended period.  
 
During a power outage event, the first back up (or remediation) is that of the dual redundant power 
supply. If this also suffers a failure, the second back up is to utilise the generators to power the site. 
Essentially, the back -up generators serve as a se condary back-up to the primary provision of a dual 
redundant power supply, and as such their use is designed to be minimal. 
 
A Technical Note has been prepared to outline the proposed back-up power supply and is provided at 
Appendix 16 . For t he purposes of th e report, only the diesel -generators are considered as back-up 
power, the UPS system and associated battery storage systems are for energy storage and short -term 
supply only.  
 
Although it is not possible to determine exactly the duration of power outage/damage over a year 
which would require the standby generators to be in operation, based on information provided by the 
electricity supplier Ausgrid it has been determined that the average unplanned actual system average 
interruption duration index (SAIDI) of power outage incident over the past 10 years is 74.0 minutes (total 
cumulative events) of unplanned outages per year per customer – based on year 2013/14 to 2023/24 of 
SAIDI d ata. This equates to just over 0.01% of likelihood of occurrence in a year. Any interruption 
without back-up power generation on Site would be of extreme detriment to the general community 
through disruption to the internet services facilitated by the data centre in the area. 
 
  
[[page 149]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  136  
6.1.10.2 Back-up power supply selection and alternatives 
 
The Proponent has opted for diesel -generator sets for the back -up power source for the Site. This is 
based on the most appropriate technology for a mission critical data centre and the market readiness 
for this technology in New South Wales. Alternatives have been considered for th e Site, but none are 
considered viable except for alternative fuel for the generator engines.  
 
6.1.10.2.1 Alternatives Considered  
 
The Proponent has investigated several options for back-up power supply technologies for data centre 
applications in terms of suitability for deployment in data centres, technological maturity in Australia, 
and viability for the Site. TABLE 21 below describes the potential alternative  back-up power supply 
technology, rates its technology maturity, and provides a recommendation on its suitability for data 
centres. As outlined in the table, the following technologies were recommended as potentially suitable 
for deployment as long duration backup supply in data centres: 
 
• Solar panels with large scale Lithium-ion batteries  
• Diesel generators with renewable diesel (HVO) as a fuel  
• Open Cycle Gas Turbines (OCGT) or Reciprocating Engines with natural gas as a fuel  
• Hydrogen Fuel Generator

6.1.10.2.2 Proposed back-up power supply 
 
The Site is designed for a total of 72 low voltage 3 MW generators for the IT load within the data halls 
and one (1) low voltage 1MW generator for the office /FOH and associated safety services. These 
generators will only operate in the event of a mains utility failure to the respective electrical block or 
for testing purposes. These generators support a total load capacity of 169MW. 
 
  
[[page 150]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  137  
Generator Plantrooms & Enclosure 
 
To improve acoustics, safety, and space efficiency, all Data Hall generators supporting the IT load will 
be housed in stand-alone six-storey, acoustically treated plantrooms within a post-tensioned reinforced 
concrete framed structure. Twelve (12) above ground bulk fuel tanks will be located on basement level 
of the generator gantry to store diesel along with associated pump sets. Each plantroom contains six 
generators.  
 
The plantrooms will include the following:  
 
• Generator Engine Set 
• LV isolator switchboard 
• 1,000L day tank 
• Control and Power Panels 
• Acoustic attenuation for cold air intake, hot air discharge (for ventilation) 
• Exhaust Gas Silencer & Flue 
 
The office/FOH generator will be housed in an acoustically treated enclosure and mounted on the roof 
of the office/FOH building. One (1) above ground bulk fuel tank will be located in the basement level in 
a dedicated tank room to store diesel along with a ssociated pump set. Fuel tanks will be designed to 
comply with AS 1940.   
 
Testing Procedures 
 
The generators will each be tested to ensure they are functional in the event of a utility failure. Initially 
all generators as they are installed at the Site will undergo commission testing which involves several 
hours that the generator will run. After the generator is installed and commissioned, regular testing will 
occur for all generators. For a breakdown of generator testing across the Site, refer to TABLE 22 below.

Generators are tested by connecting to a load bank (equipment that consumes electricity for testing 
purposes). Only one generator per gantry wing would be tested at any one time (two in total) with two 
(2) 3MW load banks being proposed on the roof of the main building to use for UPS and generator 
testing. Two (2) generato rs are proposed to run standard tests at the same time to reduce the total 
testing time of all generators per annum.
