Based on the text and statutory context of clause 17 of the POEO Act, that ‘plant’ is interpreted to 
encompass all generators on the premises collectively, and that ‘operate’ would also include testing 
should the internal combustible engines be turned on d uring testing. Applying the above testing 
schedule, the total testing time of the 73 generators will be 197.1 hours a year in total and therefore does 
not exceed the 200-hour threshold prescribed by the POEO Act as requiring an EPL.  
 
6.1.11 Air Quality   
 
An Air Quality Impact Assessment (AQIA) has been prepared by Northstar in support of the Proposal 
and is provided at  Appendix 17 . The AQIA  identifies and examines potential air quality impacts 
associated with the construction and subsequent operation of the Proposal, aligning with the SEARs 
requirements, and outlines mitigation and monitoring requirements commensurate with those 
anticipated impacts to ensure that air quality criteria are achieved at surrounding sensitive receptor 
locations. 
 
To allow assessment of the level of risk associated with the Proposal in relation to air quality, the AQIA 
has been performed in accordance with and with due reference to:  
 
• Protection of the Environment Operations Act 1997;  
• Protection of the Environment Operations (Clean Air) Regulation 2022;  
• Guidance on the Assessment of Dust from Demolition and Construction (IAQM, 2024); and,  
• Approved Methods for the Modelling and Assessment of Air Pollutants in NSW (NSW EPA, 
2022). 
 
6.1.11.1 Existing Environment 
 
The AQIA provides context around the location of sensitive receptor locations surrounding the Site, the 
prevailing meteorology and air quality of the surrounding area and identifies other sources of air 
pollutants which have the potential to impact cumulatively with the Proposal.  
 
Sensitive Receptor Locations   
 
Air quality assessments typically use a desk-top mapping study to identify ’discrete receptor locations’, 
which are intended to represent a selection of locations that may be susceptible to changes in air 
quality.  In broad terms, the identification of sensitive receptors, refers to places at which humans may 
be present for a period representative of the averaging period for the pollutant being assessed.    
 
The Approved Methods defines a sensitive receptor location to be:  
 
‘A location where people are likely to work or reside; this may include a dwelling,  
school, hospital, office or public recreational area’.  
 
It is noted that the assessment criteria applied to particulates and sulphur dioxide (SO2) (see Table 5 of 
the AQIA) are for a 24-hour averaging period, and as such the predicted impacts need to be interpreted 
at commercial and industrial receptor locations with care. It is considered to be atypical for a person to 
be at those locations for a complete 24 -hour period and as such, the exposure risks associated with 
those pollutants at those locations would be over -estimated by adoption of those locations in the 
modelling assessment.  
 
It is important to note that the selection of discrete receptor locations does not aim to cover all sensitive  
receptors within the study area. Rather, the selected locations are intended to be representative of their  
broader surroundings and can reasonably be assumed to reflect conditions in the immediate vicinity. 
In cases where multiple sensitive receptors exist in close proximity (such as a school adjacent to a 
medical centre) the receptor closest to the emission source is typically chosen to assess potential risks 
to other sensitive land uses in the area. 
[[page 152]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  139  
 
Beyond the identified discrete receptors, the entire modelling area has been overlaid with a grid of 
uniform receptor points to map predicted impacts in a comprehensive manner. As such, the accidental 
omission of a particular sensitive location does not in validate the AQIA or render it incapable of 
assessing potential risks.  
 
In accordance with the requirements of the Approved Methods, several receptors have been identified 
and the receptors adopted for use within this AQIA are presented in TABLE 23 and illustrated in  Figure 
81. TABLE 23  is not intended to represent a definitive list of sensitive land uses, but a cross section of 
available locations, that are used to characterise larger areas, or selected as they represent more 
sensitive locations, which may represent people who are more susceptible to changes in air pollution.  
 
To ensure that the selection of discrete  receptors for the AQIA are reflective of the locations in which 
the population of the area surrounding the premises reside, population density data has been 
examined. Population density data based on the 2021 census have been obtained from the Australian 
Bureau of Statistics (ABS) for a 1 square kilometre (km2) grid, covering mainland Australia (ABS, 2022). 
Using a Geographical Information System (GIS), the locations of sensitive receptor locations have been  
confirmed with reference to their population densities (refer Figure 81 below).

6.1.11.2 Assessment of Impacts 
 
The assessment evaluates the potential air quality impacts during both construction and operation 
phases, with a focus on dust soiling and increased ambient PM10 (including PM2.5) concentrations due 
to dust arising from construction activities on the Site, and combustion emissions from standby diesel 
generator engines during operations. 
 
Data from available sources, including information provided by the Applicant and publicly accessible  
environmental data, were utilised. The emergency use of diesel generators is predicted to have the 
potential to lead to the exceedance of air quality criteria at nearby sensitive receptor locations from 
associated emissions to air. The routine testing under the parameters assessed was not predicted to 
result in any exceedances of the air quality criteria at any receptor location.  
 
6.1.11.2.1 Construction Phase Risk Assessment 
 
The construction phase risk assessment for the Proposal as presented in Section 6 of the AQIA indicates 
that dust soiling impacts pose a medium risk level for earthworks, construction, trackout, and 
construction-traffic activities. In terms of human health impacts, these activities are considered to pose 
a low risk, assuming no mitigation measures are applied to control emissions.  

[[page 154]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  141  
 
Based upon that assessment, a range of mitigation measures are recommended to ensure that short -
term impacts associated with construction phase activities are minimised, as presented in Appendix B 
of the AQIA. 
 
6.1.11.2.2 Operational Phase Impact Assessment 
 
The predicted impacts of operational phase activities under a worst -case scenario (Scenario 1) and 
realistic operational scenario (Scenario 2) are presented in Section 7 of the AQIA and discussed below. 
 
Scenario 1 – Justified Worst Case Operations  
 
Under the justified worst -case standby generator operational scenario (Scenario 1), a number of 
additional exceedances of the short-term air quality criteria for NO2, PM10 and PM2.5 are predicted.   
 
That scenario assumes that all 73 generators would be operational at one time. The predicted 
incremental concentrations under Scenario 1 show exceedances of particulate matter (as PM2.5) and 
NO2 at sensitive receptor locations if a power outage occurred, and all 7 3 emergency generators were 
operating at 100% load continuously (refer Section 7.1 of the AQIA) 
 
An assessment of the probability (p) of an exceedance of the relevant short -term criteria has been 
performed and is presented in Section 7.1.4 of the AQIA. As a maximum across all receptors, the 
probability of an exceedance of the NO2 or PM2.5 criterion (where p=0 is an impossible event, and p=1 
is a certain event) in any year is:  
 
• NO2: p = 0.0014; and 
• PM2.5: p = 0.0027.  
 
To predict the likelihood of exceedances under the worst-case scenario (i.e. all 73 generators operating  
continuously at 100% load), the reliability of the power network was considered against the latest 
information supplied in the Ausgrid 2024 Distribution and Transmission Annual Planning Report 
(DTAPR) (Ausgrid, 2024).  
 
Based on the DTAPR and associated network reliability statistics, the average unplanned outage 
duration per year per customer over a 10-year period from financial-year 2013 (2013/14) to financial-year 
2023 (2023/24) equated to approximately 74.0 minutes, although the exact duration of power outages 
requiring standby generators cannot be determined. Correspondingly, the likelihood of power 
interruptions occurring is approximately 0.014 % of the time per year (74.0 / (8 760 × 60)) or have a 
probability of p=0.000141.  
 
The probability of both the interruption to the power supply, and an exceedance of the relevant air 
quality criteria occurring can be calculated through the multiplication of the probability of each event 
occurring.  
 
Those values are incredibly small and have been placed into context by calculating the percentage 
chance that the event could occur in a number of years. The results suggest a low likelihood of 
exceeding air quality criteria during a power outage.  
 
The Proposal is to be connected to a new Ausgrid precinct -wide STSS and is sited in proximity to the 
Ausgrid 132 kV transmission network, where power outages are expected to be significantly shorter 
than the network -wide average. The reported statistics in clude less reliable power lines, whereas an 
outage on the 132 kV network would result in a widespread Sydney-wide outage.  
 
  
[[page 155]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  142  
Scenario 2 – Maintenance Testing  
 
Annual average particulate matter concentrations (as TSP, PM10 and PM2.5) resulting from Scenario 2 
are predicted to comply with the relevant impact assessment criteria, with no exceedances predicted.  
 
Under Scenario 2, no additional exceedances of the cumulative impact assessment criteria for 24-hour 
PM10 and 24-hour PM2.5 are predicted as a result of the Proposal. While several exceedances of the 24-
hour PM10 and PM2.5 criteria are identified in Table 25 and Table 26 of the AQIA, these are attributable 
to elevated background concentrations that already exceed the respective criteria and are not related 
to emissions from the Proposal.  
 
For 1-hour and annual average NO2, no cumulative exceedances are predicted due to the Proposal. 
Predicted incremental and cumulative 1 -hour NO2 concentrations remain below the relevant criteria 
at all considered receptor locations.  
 
Predicted concentrations of CO, SO2, PAHs, VOCs, and CH2O across all assessed averaging periods are 
also below the relevant impact assessment criteria at all receptor locations under Scenario 2.  
 
Correspondingly, it is anticipated that under operation of the testing schedule, as outlined in Section 
2.3.2 of the AQIA, that no significant air quality impacts are predicted to be experienced at sensitive 
receptors during routine operations.  
 
Odour  
 
Operational phase activities will not result in any odour emissions, except for the periodic operation of 
the diesel-fuelled generators for testing and back-up power generation purposes, as outlined in Section 
2.3.3 of the AQIA. Toluene (C7H8) and xylene ( C8H10) emissions have been assessed and compared 
against the relevant odour impact assessment criteria. No exceedances of the relevant odour criteria 
are predicted during either emergency or realistic operations. 
 
POEO (Clean Air) Regulation – Standard of Concentrations  
 
Section 7.3 of the AQIA assesses generator emissions against the applicable POEO CAR concentration 
standards for non-scheduled activities, demonstrating compliance with the total solid particle 
standards. Although the operations may be exempt from the relevant emission limit regulations (i.e. 
in-stack emission concentrations), the Applicant would not be exempt from ensuring the emissions 
do not exceed ambient air quality criteria.  
 
6.1.11.2.3 Mitigation Measures 
 
Recommended Mitigation Measures  
 
Based on the findings of the dispersion modelling assessment under Scenario 2, it is considered that 
the operation of the testing schedule would not result in exceedances being experienced at sensitive 
receptor locations surrounding the Site. To ensure air quality impacts experienced at sensitive receptors 
resulting from the operation of the Site are minimised, maintenance under the testing schedule must 
be performed as outlined in Section 2.3.2 of the AQIA. Operation of the emergency generators should 
be minimised as far as practicably possible.  
 
Additional Mitigation Measures  
 
A number of additional mitigation measures considered to be Best Available Technology (BAT) have 
been reviewed and discussed in Appendix F of the AQIA. For clarity, the Proposal is predicted to not 
result in any exceedances of the relevant air quality crit eria under the proposed maintenance testing 
schedule (refer Section 2.2 of the AQIA).  
[[page 156]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  143  
 
Correspondingly, the additional controls discussed in Appendix F have been reviewed to solely provide 
context for how air quality impacts may be further reduced. 
 
6.1.11.2.4 Cumulative Impacts  
 
A desktop survey identified local data centres with similar emissions profiles to assess potential 
cumulative impacts. The Proposal’s cumulative air quality impacts were evaluated alongside these 
sources, including during emergency and realistic operations. 
 
A semi -quantitative probability -based analysis estimated potential cumulative impacts at sensitive 
receptors, finding a low probability of simultaneous operations or testing at nearby data centres, and 
therefore a low likelihood of cumulative impacts occurring.  
 
6.1.11.2.5 Conclusion 
 
During the construction phase, the potential dust soiling and human health risks are assessed as being  
manageable through appropriate implementation of the recommended mitigation measures. With 
the implementation of the mitigation measures as part of a Construction Environmental Management 
Plan (CEMP) that the remaining construction-phase risks associated with the Proposal are appropriate 
and acceptable.  
 
During the operational phase, based upon the information presented in th e AQIA, the normal 
operation of the Proposal is not considered likely to result in additional exceedances of the relevant air 
quality criteria at any identified receptor location. Scenarios replicating the worst -case and a realistic 
case for maintenance testing operations have been considered in the assessment.  
 
The predicted incremental concentrations for all assessed pollutants are shown to be below the 
relevant criteria under realistic operations where the back -up generators are operated under an 
appropriate testing schedule. 
 
The Proponent should ensure all mitigation measures outlined in the AQIA are adhered to and these 
measures form part of the planned management and mitigation measures in Appendix D of this EIS.  
 
6.1.12 Noise and Vibration  
 
A Noise and Vibration Impact Assessment (NVIA) has been prepared by Arup in support of the Proposal 
and is provided at  Appendix 18 . The NVIA addresses the following matters from Item 12 and the 
additional assessment requirements from the SEARs: 
 
• Provide a noise and vibration assessment prepared in accordance with the relevant EPA 
guidelines and Australian/International Standards. The assessment must detail construction 
and operational noise and vibration impacts (including testing of any back -up power 
system) on nearby sensitive receivers and structures, and outline the proposed mitigation, 
management and monitoring measures that would be implemented. 
• The EIS must include an operational noise assessment, which: 
o provide details of noise monitoring survey, background noise levels and amenity 
noise levels at the potentially most-affected residential receptors (i.e. not necessarily 
the nearest residential receptor) 
o include details of manufacturer specifications for plant and equipment and noise 
source inventory (demonstrating worst -case modelling of plant and equipment for 
each assessment period, including testing of any back-up power system and critical 
power failure scenario) 
[[page 157]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT

6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208

o assess the effect of the development on neighbouring sites within the Macquarie 
Park Corridor Precinct, particularly at the nearby key sites along Julius Avenue  
o evaluate data centre and substation operational noise for any potential annoying 
noise characteristics such as tonality and dominant low -frequency content.

6.1.12.2 Operational Noise Assessment 
 
The following operational scenarios have been adopted for the operational noise assessment, which 
represent typical worst-case conditions for each assessment period.  
 
Normal operation is defined as: 
• Day: all equipment operating including one truck movement, two generators and two load 
banks, which will operate periodically under a scheduled testing regime. 
• Evening and night: all equipment operating except the truck, generators and load banks. 
 
Criteria for normal operation is defined in Section 4.2.1 of the NVIA.  
 
Emergency operation is defined as: 
• A utility failure when all equipment is operating including all generators.  
 
Criteria for emergency operation is discussed in Section 4.2.2 of the NVIA. 
 
The noise criteria, modelling methodology, meteorological conditions, and noise predictions are 
outlined in Section 4 of the NVIA.   
 
Predicted noise emissions levels  
 
Noise predictions for under standard and noise-enhancing meteorological conditions are presented as 
follows:  
 
• TABLE 25: Day period, including testing of two generators under full load. It is noted that 
predictions include noise contributions from the two loudest generators relative to each 
receiver location. 
• TABLE 26: Evening period for normal operations 
• TABLE 27: Night period for normal operation  
• TABLE 28: Emergency operations.  
 
The result tables include modifying factor adjustments as follows:  
 
• Noise data for primary noise equipment has been analysed for low frequency and tonal noise 
characteristics in Appendix E.2.4 and Appendix E.2.5. of the NVIA.  
• Site noise emissions are not expected to exhibit tonal characteristics.   
• Low frequency noise has been assessed in accordance with the Tables C1 and C2 of the NPfI 
and the best practice approach for applying the low frequency noise analysis as detailed in the 
journal paper prepared by the NSW Environment Protection Authority published by Acoustics 
Australia [19]. This analysis is presented in Appendix F. Modifying factor corrections for low 
frequency were not required to be applied for assessed receivers.   
• Intermittent noise is not expected from the proposal as the noise sources are steady -state in 
nature.  
 
Predicted noise levels at all sensitive receivers have been assessed against criteria in Section 4.2.1 of the 
NVIA during standard and noise-enhancing weather conditions.  
 
Noise contours at a height of 2 metres relative to ground level are presented in Appendix G of the NVIA 
for each of the normal operating scenarios. Vertical façade noise maps of the nearest future apartment 
buildings are also presented in Appendix G of the NVIA.

6.1.12.3 Road Traffic Noise 
 
Access to and from the site will be via Julius Avenue which is a local road with traffic generally coming 
from Delhi Road. It is noted land uses along Julius Avenue generally consist of commercial premises 
with some childcare centres and future residential apartments 
 
Due to the anticipated low number of vehicles generated by the site (up to 5 trucks per day), existing 
high traffic volumes on Delhi Road and Julius Avenue, generated traffic noise is anticipated to comply 
with the NSW Road Noise Policy. 
 
6.1.12.4 Construction Noise and Vibration Assessment 
 
An assessment of construction noise impact from the construction works has been undertaken. Noise 
emissions from the proposed construction works have been predicted and assessed against the 
relevant noise management levels set by the NSW Interim Construction Noise Guideline (ICNG) (DECC 
2009) and Assessing Vibration: a technical guideline (EPA. 2006) during the recommended standard 
hours for construction.  
 
Construction activities likely to be conducted for the project are: 
 
• Activity 1: Site establishment and excavation 
• Activity 2: Foundations 
• Activity 3: Pavement and road works 
• Activity 4: Building construction. 
• Activity 5: Plant installation and connection 
 
Predicted construction noise levels at the surrounding receiver are presented in TABLE 29.    
 
A maximum range of noise levels have been predicted for receivers located the closest to the Site as 
the construction noise levels at those receivers will vary greatly depending on location of equipment.  
 
The lower level assumes that all construction equipment i s operating concurrently within the Site 
which represents the maximum average noise levels at the receivers throughout the construction 
period while the upper noise levels in the range represents the maximum noise levels at the receivers 
when the noisiest piece of equipment operates the closest to the receivers.   
 
It is noted that the predicted construction noise levels are generally conservative and do not represent 
a constant noise emission that would be experienced by the community on a daily basis throughout 
the project construction period. In reality the predicted noise levels will vary in intensity and would only 
be experienced for limited periods of time when works are occurring.

6.1.13 Ground and Water Conditions   
 
6.1.13.1 Groundwater Impact Assessment 
 
A Groundwater Impact Assessment (GIA) has been prepared and is provided at  Appendix 20 . The 
purpose of the GIA is to undertake an assessment of potential surface water and groundwater quality 
impacts related to the Proposal and, as necessary, outlines measures to avoid, minimise, manage, 
mitigate, offset and/or monitor the potential impacts during construction and operation of the 
Proposal.   
 
The geotechnical investigation identified that the Site is generally underlain by fresh, unbroken, 
medium to high strength Hawkesbury Sandstone. Three groundwater monitoring wells were installed 
for the project, BH101A, BH103, and BH106. Groundwater levels were steady throughout the monitoring 
period, ranging between 35 and 29 m AHD, sloping south -east following the nearby topography . The 
groundwater monitoring wells are shown in Figure 83 below.  
 
[[page 170]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  157  
 
Figure 83. Test Location Plan (Source: Douglas Partners, 2025) 
 
The proposed bulk excavation level for the Proposal is understood to be RL 30 m AHD, and is therefore 
expected to intersect the groundwater table, which is likely to be associated with minor seepage along 
the top of the rock and through fractures in the rock. 
 
Groundwater modelling undertaken for the Proposal predicts that groundwater inflow rates will be 
low (less than 1 ML/year estimated under baseline conditions). Results of the sensitivity scenarios also 
suggest results below 1 ML/year although higher flow r ates could occur during prolonged wetter 
periods. These predictions are estimates based on the information available; actual flow rates may vary 
from these and will only be known once the excavation is complete and inflow rates can be measured.  
 
Given that the predicted inflow is less than 3 ML/year under typical observed conditions, the proposed 
excavation may be eligible for a Water Access Licence exemption from WaterNSW , subject to review 
and approval by WaterNSW. 
 
From a geotechnical point of view, it is considered that a drained basement is feasible without any 
significant impact to surrounding properties. In addition, the use of a drained basement is not 
considered likely to influence any Groundwater Dependent Ecosystems (GDEs) or groundwater supply 
works in accordance with the NSW Aquifer Interference Policy (AIP) minimal impact considerations. 
 
Groundwater quality results suggested that some background metal and total recoverable 
hydrocarbons (TRH) concentrations exceed the adopted guideline values. Disposal criteria should be 
selected prior to dewatering based on background concentrations and relevant guideline values. 
Should disposal criteria be exceeded, treatment may be required prior to disposal. Long term disposal 
options for drained water should be considered.

6.1.13.1.1 Mitigation Measures

continued one month after construction. The monitoring and reporting program  is referenced in the 
Mitigation measures Table provided at Appendix D.  
 
6.1.13.2 Water Management 
 
A Water Management Plan has been prepared by TTW and is provided at  Appendix 21. The report 
provides a summary of the stormwater management design for the Proposal. Key components of the 
stormwater management system are discussed below: 
 
On-Site Detention (OSD) 
 
Stormwater has been designed in accordance with the RDCP 2014 and the Stormwater Management 
Technical Manual 2014. RDCP 2014 prescribes that outflows from the Site for all design storms are 
required to be less than the maximum outflow for the uncontrolled post -development site in the 5 -
year Annual Recurrence Interval (ARI) which is equivalent to the 20% Annual Exceedance Probability 
(AEP) storm event. 
 
Council have confirmed that the proposed OSD system must only attenuate and maintain the 5 -year 
ARI (20% AEP) pre -development flows for the portion of Site being redeveloped. Thus, the southern 
portions of Site being maintained as dense forested land can be omitted from the Permissible Site 
Discharge (PSD) calculation and modelling. The Proposal covers an area of approximately 20,750sqm, 
which is about 7,880msqm less than the overall Site area. 
 
The Proposal will include two  (2) OSD tanks to meet the discharge requirements set by Council. The 
first tank, located within the basement, has an effective storage volume of 524 cbm and discharges 
toward the southwest side of the proposed access road. The second tank, with an effective volume of 
131cbm positioned adjacent to the STSS and MSB room, discharging to the eastern area near 
Richardson Place. Both proposed OSD tank outlets will discharge through level spreaders. The 
proposed OSD and drainage plan has been modelled through the program DRAINS in line with Council 
requirements. 
 
DRAINS Modelling 
 
The preliminary DRAINS model illustrates the flow dynamics from both the uncontrolled post -
development catchment and the post-development catchment with OSD systems, as well as to review 
the overland flow regime in for capacity and adequacy in the major st orm event. The OSDs have been 
designed to capture runoff from all roof catchments as well as the hardstand and landscaped areas. 
The eastern end of the access road has been treated as bypassing the proposed OSD tanks. The 
stormwater catchment plan is indicatively shown in Figure 84 below for the developed area of the Site. 
 
[[page 172]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  159  
 
Figure 84. Stormwater Catchment Plan (Source: TTW, 2025) 
 
Based on the DRAINS model, the 20% AEP outflow from the uncontrolled post -development 
catchment is 416 L/s, which represents the permissible site discharge (PSD). The outflows from the OSD 
systems during the 1% AEP storm are 252 L/s and 70 L/s, with an additional 89 L/s bypass. This results in 
a total site discharge of 411  L/s. As this total discharge remains below the PSD, the proposed design is 
considered compliant and acceptable. 
 
Stormwater Quality Management 
 
Stormwater quality treatment is required to comply with the requirements outlined in Part 8.2 - 
Stormwater and Floodplain Management of RDCP 2014. Within this, water quality treatment devices 
on Site must achieve the water quality targets outlined in Section 3.3.1.b of the Stormwater  
and Floodplain Management guide which are reproduced as follows: 
 
• Gross Pollutants – 90% 
• Total Suspended Solids – 85% 
• Total Phosphorus – 60% 
• Total Nitrogen – 45% 
 
Stormwater quality reduction targets and maintenance of treatment measures are proposed to be met 
through a combination of the following treatment options: 
 
▪ Constructed Wetlands (preferred over bioretention basins and ponds) 
▪ Operation and Maintenance Plan 
▪ Trash racks (preferred over GPTs) 
▪ Ocean Protect StormFilter Cartridges (or equivalent) 
▪ Ocean Protect Oceanguard Pit Inserts (or equivalent) 

[[page 173]]

6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208

▪ Rainwater reuse tanks 
▪ Swales, bioretention swales and buffer strip 
▪ Bioretention basins 
▪ Raingardens

Conclusion 
 
The Water Management Plan concludes  that the Proposal is suitable, will not result in unacceptable 
impact, and warrants approval subject to implementation of the following proposed mitigation 
measures: 
 
▪ A site-wide in-ground stormwater pits and pipes network; 
▪ Two on-site stormwater detention (OSD) tanks with combined volume of 655m3to reduce the 
discharge from the site to the permissible site discharge allowed in the RDCP 2014 Part 8.2: 
Stormwater Management Technical Manual; 
▪ Stormwater treatment measures including 10 x OceanProtect Oceanguards (or similar 
approved equivalent) and 21 x 690mm Ocean Protect PSorb StormFilters (or similar approved 
equivalent) to meet the pollutant reduction targets set out in the RDCP 2014 Part 8.2: 
Stormwater Management Technical Manual 
▪ A wastewater design in alignment with the Sydney Water mandated requirements; 
▪ A firewater design in alignment with Fire and Rescue NSW requirements; and 
▪ An erosion and sediment control plan to manage stormwater quality and quantity on site 
during the construction phase of the development 
 
6.1.13.3 Geotechnical Investigations 
 
A Geotechnical Investigation has been prepared by Douglas Partners and is included at Appendix 19. 
The aim of the investigation was to provide geotechnical comments on site preparation, excavation, 
anchoring, excavation support, groundwater and foundation .. The conclusions of the field work and  
recommendations are discussed in summary below.  
 
Excavation Conditions 
 
It is understood that bulk excavation down to approximately RL 30m  bulk excavation level (BEL) will 
be required across the existing platform as part of the Proposal. Trafficability of the sandstone at BEL is 
not expected to be problematic for tracked general construction machinery. Should heavy plant be 
required, such as piling rigs or very heavy cranes, a working platform is likely to be required, and an 
assessment should be carried out based on the approach set out by the Federation of Piling Specialist 
(UK). 
 
Excavation Support  
 
Excavated faces in medium and high strength sandstone are generally self -supporting, except where 
affected by adversely oriented joints. Rock wedges or feather edges (sub-parallel joints intersecting the 
face) may occur particularly along the east and west faces of excavations where the predominant NNE-
SSW steeply dipping joints are expected. Note that these joints can still be encountered in the rock 
faces even though they have not been encountered in the boreholes.  
 
During excavation, it is recommended that all rock faces are inspected by a geotechnical engineer to 
confirm that the site conditions are consistent with design assumptions and to verify the stability of 
the faces and advise on any support requirements. It  is recommended that the excavations are 
inspected every 1.5 m drop in excavation height or every 1.0 m drop where excavation is extending 
below adjacent foundations or structures.  
 
Note that rock mass support can only be finalised once the actual joint location, strike and dip have 
been determined by the geotechnical engineer/engineering geologist during excavation. 
 
  
[[page 175]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  162  
Foundations  
 
The geotechnical model indicates medium strength and high strength sandstone is expected at the 
new footing excavation levels. The design of pad or strip footings on medium and high strength 
sandstone may be carried out using the values given in Table 9 of the Geotechnical Investigation.  
 
Dilapidation Surveys and Monitoring  
 
Dilapidation surveys should be carried out on adjacent buildings, pavements, services and any sensitive 
structures that may be affected by the excavation works. A baseline (reference) survey should be carried 
out before the commencement of any excavation w ork in order to document existing defects so that 
any claims for damage due to construction related activities can be accurately assessed. 
 
Further Investigation  
 
At the time of the investigation, areas on the Site were unable to be accessed and some planned 
boreholes were unable to be completed. The Geotechnical Investigation recommends the bores in the 
vicinity of the proposed driveway/access road (dense bushland currently) are still carried out, at a time 
when they are accessible.  
 
Mapping of all exposed rock faces will be required and a rock face stability assessment carried out. 
During construction phase, if the groundwater monitoring wells that have been installed are destroyed 
during the basement excavation, new wells will need t o be installed outside the excavation or in safe 
locations. 
 
6.1.13.4 Salinity Management 
 
Reference to the Map of Salinity Potential in Western Sydney indicates the site is not situated within 
the map boundaries and is therefore not in an area with potential for dryland salinity. Accordingly, the 
preparation of a Salinity Management Plan is not warranted for the Site.  
 
6.1.13.5 Acid Sulfate Soils Management 
 
The Site is identified on the RLEP 2014 Acid Sulfate Soils Map as Class 5 Acid Sulfate Soils. A preliminary 
assessment indicates that the potential for acid sulfate soils to be present at the Site is considered to 
be low and it is not thought to present a constraint to the Proposal. Accordingly, the preparation of. an 
Acid Sulfate Soils Management Plan is not warranted for the Site.  
 
6.1.14 Flooding Risk  
 
A Flood Impact and Risk Assessment (FIRA) has been prepared by TTW and is provided at  Appendix 
22. The FIRA has been prepared to address the following SEARs requirements: 
 
• Identify the flood planning level as set out in the relevant council LEP or SEPP and identify 
any: 
o flood risks on site having regard to adopted flood studies 
o the potential effects of climate change, and 
o any relevant provisions of the NSW Flood Risk Management Manual. 
• Where the development is occurring on flood prone land a flood impact and risk assessment 
(FIRA) must be prepared having regard to the Flood Impact and Risk Assessment Guideline 
- LU01 (FIRA guide). When determining the scope and category of the FIRA the requirements 
outlined in the FIRA guide must be considered. 
• Detail any flood risk management measures that are  to be incorporated as part  of the 
development having regard to relevant guidelines (including any design solutions, flood 
[[page 176]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  163  
modification measures, property modification measures, operational procedures or Flood 
Emergency Response Plan). 
 
The FIRA confirms that the Site is identified as a low flood risk zone, with the predominant flood hazard 
classified as H1 (see Figure 86 below) - indicating conditions that are generally safe for people, vehicles, 
and buildings. 
 
 
Figure 86. 1% AEP Flood Hazard (ZAEM1)- Existing Scenario (Source: TTW, 2025) 
 
Flood modelling has been completed using the updated TUFLOW hydraulic model (2025.0.0-iSP-w64) 
to improve reliability and address previous anomalies. The model incorporated existing and post -
development Site conditions, as well as climate change scenarios, blockage sensitivity tests, and 
Probable Maximum Flood (PMF) events. The results indicate that the majority of the site experiences 
shallow flooding (generally <50  mm) with low velocities (<0.5  m/s), and that no significant afflux is 
generated by the development. Localised afflux within the Site boundary is attributed to increases in 
surface elevation in the post -development digital terrain model and does not reflect true hydraulic 
impact. 
 
Key flood mitigation measures include: 
 
• Installation of two new drainage lines on the eastern and western sides of the Site. 
• Four additional stormwater pits along Julius Avenue. 
• Upgrading three drainage pipes to 525 mm diameter. 
• Construction of a 0.5 m high wall adjacent to the cul-de-sac to the south to reduce local afflux. 
 
The finished floor level of the main building entrance is 48.0  m AHD. The 1% AEP and PMF flood level 
at this location is 47.60  m AHD. The higher of the two flood planning levels  - either 1% AEP + 300  mm 
or PMF—is 47.90 m AHD, which remains below the adopted finished floor level. 
 

[[page 177]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  164  
Based on the modelling outcomes and proposed mitigation measures, the Proposal is demonstrated 
to have no adverse flood impacts on adjacent or downstream properties and satisfies both Council and 
SEARs flood risk requirements. The Site can be developed and operated safely, with appropriate design 
measures to manage residual flood risk. 
 
A separate Flood Emergency Response Plan (FERP) will be prepared for the Proposal to address Site -
specific flood safety and operational procedures. This is included in the Mitigation measures Table 
provided at Appendix D of this EIS.  
 
6.1.15 Hazards and Risks  
 
A Preliminary Risk Screening (PRS) has been prepared in support of the Proposal and is provided at  
Appendix 23. The PRS has been completed in accordance with the Resilience and Hazards SEPP and 
Applying SEPP 33  - Hazardous and Offensive Development Application Guidelines (Applying SEPP 
33). Under the Resilience and Hazards SEPP, the PRS stage assesses the storage and transport of 
specific dangerous goods classes that have the potential for significant, off-site effects.  Specifically, this 
stage involves the identification of classes and quan tities of all dangerous goods to be used, stored or 
produced on Site with respect to storage depot locations as well as transported to and from the Site.   
 
6.1.15.1 Diesel 
 
The Proposal seeks consent for the storage of 1,272,000 L (1,081 tonnes) of diesel. This is distributed as 
follows: 
 
• Bulk Storage (12 x 100 kL tanks) 
• Generator Service Tanks (72 x 1 kL tanks) 
 
Diesel fuel to be stored on Site is not classed as a Dangerous Goods, but is classed as a C1 Combustible 
Liquid, provided no flammable liquids are stored with the diesel. The Proponent has confirmed that no 
flammable liquids will be stored with the diesel  and therefore the diesel will be classed as a C1 
Combustible Liquid.   
 
Note that C1 combustible liquids are not a dangerous good under UN (United Nations) classification. 
However, they are defined as dangerous goods under NSW workplace legislation. Where dangerous 
goods are used or stored in volumes greater than the manifest quantities specified in schedule 11 of the 
Work Health and Safety Regulation 2017, Safework NSW must be notified, which will include manifests 
and lodgement an emergency plan to Fire and Rescue NSW. Further advice on these requirements 
should be sort from Safework NSW.   
 
It should be noted that the Protection of the Environment Operations Act 1997, Schedule 1, Clause 9(1) 
indicates that ‘petroleum products storage’, which would include diesel fuel storage, is a Scheduled 
Activity. Capacity to store greater than 2,000 tonnes requires an environment protection licence under 
the Protection of the Environment Operations Act 1997 (POEO Act), from the NSW EPA.   
 
The proposed inventory of diesel and classification is provided in TABLE 30 below.

6.1.15.2 Batteries (UPS) 
 
The Proposal seeks consent for the storage of 35,280 kg of Lithium-Ion batteries (72 battery cabinets x 
490kg per cabinet).  
 
Class 9 products are miscellaneous dangerous goods, which are generally considered to pose little 
threat to people or property in their stable state.  
 
They may be substances which pose an environmental hazard, and the consent authority should 
consider whether or not a potential for environmental harm exists. 
 
It is acknowledged however for lithium-ion batteries that the potential of thermal runaway effects may 
lead to increased fire hazards under certain conditions. As stipulated by the consent authority, and as  
documented by the development team, the project installation is to comply with the following, to 
minimise potential for environmental harm: 
 
• Factory Mutual Global Property Loss Prevention Data Sheet (FMDS) 5-32 – Data Centres and 
Related Facilities 
• FMDS 5-33 – Lithium-Ion Battery Energy Storage Systems 
• AS/NZS 4681 – Storage and handling of Class 9 (miscellaneous) dangerous goods articles 
• AS IEC 62619 – Secondary cells and batteries containing alkaline or other non -acid 
electrolytes – Safety requirements for secondary lithium cells and batteries, for use in 
industrial applications. 
 
It is also noted that a Fire Safety Study will be developed, in accordance with HIPAP 2 – FSS Guidelines  
(Hazardous Industry Planning Advisory Paper No 2), in order to identify hazards, consequences and 
adequately mitigate risks 
 
6.1.15.3 Dangerous Goods Transport 
 
Dangerous Goods are not transported for the operation of the Site. Therefore, the Proposal is not 
considered potentially hazardous with regards to transport of Dangerous Goods. 
 
6.1.15.4 Conclusion 
 
The Applying SEPP 33 preliminary risk screening for storage of dangerous goods indicate that the 
Proposal may not be classified as a hazardous or offensive industry.    
 
The volume of diesel fuel storage to be stored at the Site does exceed the manifest quantities for C1 
combustible liquids specified in schedule 11 of the Work Health and Safety Regulation 2017.  
 
Therefore, the Safework NSW notification will need to be notified, which will include manifests and 
lodgement an emergency plan to Fire and Rescue NSW.    
 
Under the POEO Act, Schedule 1, Clause 9(1) ‘petroleum products storage’, which would include diesel 
fuel storage, is a Scheduled Activity. Capacity to store greater than 2,000 tonnes require an 
environment protection licence from the NSW Environmental Protection Authority (EPA). Under the 
current design the storage capacity of 1,272,000 L (1,081 tonnes) proposed for Site, the Proposal will not 
require an EPL.   
 
[[page 179]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  166  
The hazards associated with lithium-ion batteries and the control measures to be put in place will be 
set out in the Fire Safety Study.  
 
It is the conclusion of the PRS that the Proposal is not  considered to be an offensive or hazardous 
development on the Site.      
 
6.1.16 Contamination and Remediation   
 
A Detailed Site Investigation (DSI) has been prepared for the Site and are provided at Appendix 24. The 
purpose of the DSI was to assess the suitability of the Site for the Proposal and whether further 
investigation and / or management of contamination is required, and to assess the potential for 
contamination migration off-site. 
 
The following key guidelines were consulted in the preparation of the DSI: 
 
• NEPC National Environment Protection (Assessment of Site Contamination) Measure 1999 (as 
amended 2013) [NEPM] (NEPC, 2013); and 
• NSW EPA Guidelines for Consultants Reporting on Contaminated Land (NSW EPA, 2020) 
 
6.1.16.1 Findings 
 
The reviewed site history documents indicate the Site was historically undeveloped prior to being 
acquired for assumed research purpose under the Commonwealth government in the 1940s, and then 
for research by CSIRO. Various Site structures (large commercial / industrial style buildings) were 
previously present in the northern portion of the Site, assumed to be used for research purposes, and 
have since been demolished. The northern portion of the Site has subsequently been excavated to 
sandstone bedrock (circa 2010), with no identified signs of contamination observed by Douglas in the 
sandstone exposed at the ground surface. The southern portion of the Site appears to have remained 
as undeveloped bushland throughout the known history of the Site.  
 
The investigation comprised fifteen shallow boreholes drilled using hand tools until natural soils were 
encountered or prior to refusal. The investigation also comprised a groundwater component, sampling 
from the groundwater wells installed as part of the geotechnical investigation.  
 
The sampling density for this investigation was reduced based on an assessed low risk of soil 
contamination based on the northern portion of the Site had been excavated into sandstone, and that 
the southern portion of the Site has not been developed. The analytical results for soil samples were 
within the adopted Site assessment criteria taking into consideration of the results of the silica gel clean 
up testing. No asbestos was detected in the samples collected.  
 
Parts of the southern portion of the Site were inaccessible due to the relatively dense bushland, 
however, the Site history and available testing results indicate a generally low risk of contamination in 
these areas. Metals and TRH were recorded in groundwater above the adopted Site assessment criteria. 
Whilst some the metals appeared to be consistent with expected ambient concentrations, the lead 
and TRH may be sourced from historic activities at or upgradient of the Site.  
 
The contaminant concentrations are not considered to pose a risk to future  Site users, however may 
pose a risk to ecological receptors in the Lane Cove River or from water extracted as part of any future 
dewatering.  
 
6.1.16.2 Recommendations 
 
Based on the results of the DSI it is considered that contamination at the Site does not pose an 
unacceptable risk to future Site users under the Proposal, subject to following mitigation measures: 
 
[[page 180]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  167  
• Further monitoring of groundwater to inform assessment of the significance of these results, 
the potential for off - Site impacts on Lane Cove River, and treatment that may be required 
during any future dewatering. 
• Preparation and implementation of an unexpected finds protocol during redevelopment to 
set out the management requirements in the event that unexpected contamination is 
identified (e.g. fly tipping of contaminated soils and localised fill). 
 
6.1.16.3 Management and/or Mitigation Measures  
 
Subject to the suitable implementation of the measures described in the recommendation of the DSI, 
it is concluded that the risks associated with the contamination identified can be managed such that 
human health and the environment are appropriately protected, and the Site can be made suitable for 
the proposed use on this basis.

6.1.17.4 Cumulative Impacts 
 
The projected total quantities of waste and recyclables are insignificant compared to the quantities of 
waste and recyclables generated in Sydney every day. Allowance has been made for the separation of 
recyclables as much as possible. It is possible that further separation and recovery of waste materials 
will be made during the operational phase of the development. 
 
The waste and recycling vehicle movements anticipated to be required each month are insignificant 
compared to the number of vehicle movements undertaken in this industrial area every day. 
 
6.1.17.5 Management and/or Mitigation Measures  
 
As the waste -related impact of the data centre is negligible, no mitigating measures are proposed 
beyond detailed in the WMP.  
 
6.1.18 Aboriginal Cultural Heritage   
 
An Aboriginal Cultural Heritage Assessment Report (ACHAR) was prepared by GML Heritage in support 
of the Proposal and is provided at  Appendix 26. The ACHAR has been prepared in accordance with  
several guidelines and policies, including the Guide to Determining and Issuing Aboriginal Heritage 
Impact Permits (DECC NSW 2009), the Guide to Investigating, Assessing and Reporting on Aboriginal 
Cultural Heritage in NSW (OEH 2011) and The Burra Charter: The Australia ICOMOS Charter for Places of 
Cultural Significance, 2013. 
 
An archaeological survey was conducted as part of the ACHAR . As required under Section 2.3 of The 
Code of Practice for Archaeological Investigation of Aboriginal Objects in NSW (DECCW 2010a), the 
Archaeological Survey provides evidence about the material traces of Aboriginal land use to support 
the conclusions and management recommendations in the ACHAR and has been prepared in 
consultation with Aboriginal community in accordance with the consultation requirements. 
 
6.1.18.1 Previously Identified First Nations Heritage and Archaeological Predictive Models 
 
An extensive search of the HNSW Aboriginal Heritage Information Management System (AHIMS) 
database was undertaken on 18 December 2024 (Search ID 961830). A total of 107 Aboriginal sites and 
no Aboriginal places were identified. While there are no registered A boriginal sites within study area, 
there are five within 250m. 
 
The closest site to the study area is closed art, artefact and shell site 45-6-1854 (LC/2 Lane Cove 2 Epping 
Road Bridge Ryde 012), approximately 20m southeast of the south boundary of the study area , as 
shown in Figure 87 below.  
 
[[page 184]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  171  
 
Figure 87. Detail of AHIMS results (Source: GML Heritage, 2025) 
 
Aboriginal heritage predictive modelling provides an understanding of Aboriginal sites, places and 
object distribution within a wider landscape. Based on examination of the environmental context and 
local AHIMS record, it is likely that if Aboriginal objects are or were present within the study area, they 
would most likely take the form of open artefact sites (isolated finds or low-density scatters) or shelters 
with Aboriginal cultural heritage, i.e. artefacts and/or art. 
 
The extent of the disturbance to the Site as a result of post -1788 land use may have reduced the 
capacity for the soil profile to retain in situ Aboriginal stone objects. It is possible that any remaining 
exposed sandstone platforms could present evidence for engraved art. However, given the s mall size 
of the site, coupled with the extent of development, statistically it is unlikely that such art would be 
present.  
 
The study area should be considered to have nil archaeological sensitivity in areas of deep excavation 
in the north of the study area, low to nil sensitivity on previously developed areas outside of the deep 
excavation zone, and low archaeological sensitiv ity within the south of the study area, where impacts 
have predominantly been limited to vegetation clearance.

during the assessment process. This contributed to an understanding of the local perspective on 
Aboriginal habitation and subsistence patterns, as well as the local intangible values and connections 
associated with the cultural landscape.

consultation process. The outcomes of this consultation underwrite the heritage assessment.

Copies of the final report will be sent to all RAPs. Furthermore, any future work relating to the Aboriginal 
archaeological mitigation, or any significant changes to the Proposal, will include consultation with the 
relevant Aboriginal stakeholders. 
 
6.1.18.3 Aboriginal Heritage Significance Assessment  
 
An archaeological survey for the assessment was undertaken on 3 March 2025. The four groups who 
had provided a response to the project methodology were invited to attend. Representatives Darleen 
Johnson (Murra Bidgee Mullangari Aboriginal Corporation), Ethan Trewlynn (Long Gully Cultural 
Services), Marbuck Khan (Kamilaroi Yankuntjatjara Working Group) and a site officer from Anonymous 
RAP Group #1 attended the survey. The survey aimed to identify any Aboriginal objects or sites within 
the study area, inspect current site conditions, and assess the archaeological potential of the study area. 
 
[[page 187]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  174  
The survey was conducted on foot, with the aim of observing current site conditions and identifying 
any Aboriginal sites, places, or zones of potential for subsurface deposits. The survey was conducted on 
foot to assess current site conditions and identify any Aboriginal sites or areas with potential for 
subsurface cultural deposits. The northern part of the study area consisted of an artificially levelled 
landform, with up to 15 –20m of sandstone bedrock removed below Julius Avenue. This depth 
decreased towards the south as the land sloped naturally toward the Lane Cove River. Visibility in this 
area was generally good, although lower-lying, boggy sections with dense reeds reduced visibility. The 
ground was mostly covered by sparse young trees growing in pa le, sandy soil derived from weathered 
sandstone. Frequent bedrock exposures showed signs of mechanical excavation. Survey participants 
concluded that extensive ground disturbance in this northern zone had eliminated the potential for 
Aboriginal archaeological material. 
 
The southern portion was heavily vegetated, primarily with native regrowth and invasive lantana, 
resulting in low visibility and difficult access. The landform here remained largely intact, forming a 
stepped slope with exposed sandstone outcrops. These out crops were inspected for engraved 
Aboriginal art or suitable shelter formations, but none were found. The soils, where visible, were sandy 
and pale greyish -brown. Disturbances included earthworks, likely from the 2000s, and sandstone 
debris from earlier cl earance activities. Semi -formal walking tracks with dirt paths and carved 
sandstone steps were observed, mainly along the southern boundary. Due to dense vegetation and the 
stepped terrain, no clear sightlines to the Lane Cove River were identified. No Abo riginal sites or areas 
with archaeological potential were found within the study area. 
 
Two (2) known Aboriginal sites outside the study area were inspected. Site 45 -6-1854, a shelter with a 
red ochre motif, was relocated near a walking track and lies on a steeper slope conducive to shelter 
formation—unlike the gentler slope of the study area. Site 45 -6-2599, excavated in 1997, has been 
heavily impacted by the development at 3 Richardson Place and no longer retains its natural setting. 
 
6.1.18.4 Aboriginal Heritage Significance Assessment  
 
An assessment of aesthetic and social/spiritual values of Aboriginal cultural significance can only be 
made by the relevant Aboriginal community because Aboriginal people are the primary source of 
information about their cultural heritage values. 
 
Consultation with the RAPs, investigation into the background history of the study area and local 
region, and the survey have facilitated the development of an understanding of the key social, historic 
and scientific values associated with the sites and ob jects within the study area.  Following OEH 2011 , 
values are graded in accordance with a basic ranking of high, moderate or low. The ranking is based 
upon the research potential, representativeness, rarity and educational potential of each value. 
 
• Social Value 
The study area holds social value to the Wallumedegal people and broader First Nations 
community as a small component of the wider cultural landscape. This cultural landscape is 
associated with the Holocene Lane Cove River, its tidal waters and slopes. While no Aboriginal 
sites were identified within the study area, the presence of multiple registered Aboriginal sites 
within 200m of the study area attests to the use of the sloping landforms by First Nations 
people. The lower vegetated portions of the study area meet this criterion at the moderate 
level. 
 
• Historical Value 
There are no known post -contact historical associations with the study area that have been 
identified through historical research or through consultation with First Nations communities 
to date. The study area does not meet this criterion. 
 
  
[[page 188]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  175  
• Scientific Value 
No Aboriginal sites have been identified within the study area to date, and the study area is 
assessed as holding nil Aboriginal archaeological potential within the highly impacted north 
of the study area, and broadly low potential in the less impacted sou th of the study area. As 
such, the study area has low to no research value. The study area does not meet this criterion. 
 
• Aesthetic Value 
The impacted north of the study area holds low to no aesthetic value due to the extent of past 
impacts. The south of the study area holds aesthetic values associated with the natural sloping 
landform and native vegetation. The study area meets this criterion at the moderate level. 
 
6.1.18.5 Conclusion 
 
The ACHAR concludes that no further archaeological assessment, including archaeological test 
excavation is recommended for the study area based on the assessment of archaeological potential 
within the ACHAR, and as no Aboriginal sites or objects have been identified within the study area. As 
such, the ACHAR concludes that works may proceed with caution, subject to the mitigation measures 
outlined below.  
 
6.1.18.6 Mitigation Measures  
 
The following recommendations are made in the conclusion of the ACHAR which have been 
documented within the planned management and mitigation measures in Appendix D of this EIS.  
 
Works may proceed with caution, with regard to the proposed management strategies below. 
 
• Recommendation 1 – Impact Avoidance  
 
Avoidance of heritage sites represents the best heritage outcome as it means no impact to the 
identified heritage features. No Aboriginal sites or objects have been identified within the study 
area, and the study area is considered to have nil Aboriginal a rchaeological potential in the 
north of the study area, and broadly low potential in the south of the study area. As the study 
area does not contain an identified Aboriginal heritage item or zone of high archaeological 
potential, the general strategy of avoidance is not applicable within the study area.  
 
However, care should be taken to avoid indirect impacts to AHIMS ID 45 -6-1854, a shelter site 
located approximately 20m south of the study area. This site holds cultural value to the First 
Nations community. Works upslope within the study area should be mindful of the shelter site. 
Ideally a suitable vegetation buffer, upslope of the shelter within the southernmost portion of 
the study area, should be retained to maintain the natural setting of the shelter. Significant 
excavation works which could impact th e sandstone bedrock through vibrations are not 
proposed in proximity to this shelter. Should the proposal change, a management strategy 
should be implemented to limit and monitor vibration impacts as this would be a direct 
impact to this AHIMS site. 
 
• Recommendation 2 – Heritage Induction 
 
A heritage induction should be presented to all contractors, consultants and NSW Government 
staff involved in works within the study area. The induction should be provided prior to the 
commencement of works as part of the general site induction for all wor kers. This heritage 
induction would be prepared by a suitably qualified archaeologist. The induction would detail 
the management of First Nations cultural heritage and archaeology within the study area, 
through the following: 
 
  
[[page 189]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  176  
o detailing the actual and potential Aboriginal heritage resource within the study area;  
o summarising the heritage legislation applicable to the project;  
o outlining any consent conditions related to Aboriginal cultural heritage applicable to 
the development works;  
o detailing the approach to Aboriginal heritage management in place for the project, 
including the unexpected finds procedure (UFP) in place for the site and procedure in 
case of a heritage breach; and  
o providing contact details for key project staff responsible for management of 
unexpected finds and heritage within the site. 
 
• Recommendation 3 – Unexpected finds protocol (UFP) 
 
A UFP should be developed for the project which would stipulate the process for managing 
actual or suspected Aboriginal objects (i.e. stone artefacts, cultural features, etc) identified 
during the proposed works. The procedure should be provided to all staff and contractors who 
are working on the project. The UFP should include the following measures:  
o In the instance of an unexpected find(s), works must cease immediately in the area, 
and no further disturbance can occur until the area has been assessed by a suitably 
qualified archaeologist.  
o The project archaeologist must be contacted to inspect the item(s).  
o Should the item(s) be Aboriginal in origin, the project archaeologist will advise on how 
to proceed. Works cannot recommence in the area of the unexpected find(s) until sign-
off has been given by the archaeologist. This may involve consultation with projec t 
RAPs and HNSW to discuss the management of the Aboriginal object(s).  
o In the unlikely event that human remains are discovered at any time during the works, 
works must cease immediately in the surrounding area. The findings would need to be 
immediately reported to the NSW Police and/or the NSW Coroner’s Office. MLALC and 
all project RAPs should also be contacted in the event that human remains of actual 
or suspected Aboriginal origin are identified. 
 
6.1.19 Environmental Heritage  
 
A Heritage Due Diligence Assessment (DD) was carried out by GML Heritage in support of the Proposal 
and is provided at Appendix 27 . The purpose of the Heritage DD was to: 
 
• Understand the Site history from readily available historical sources; 
• Understand the current Site conditions from a review of existing documentation; 
• Identify is there is potential for historical archaeological remains within the study area; 
• Identify if the Proposal could impact on heritage items as defined by the Heritage Act; and 
• Make recommendations regarding the management of heritage and archaeological resources 
for the Proposal.  
 
The DD included an archaeological assessment and review of nearby heritage items and the following 
provides a summary of the results as part of the Heritage DD. 
 
6.1.19.1 Impact Assessment 
 
Archaeological Impacts 
 
The DD concludes that the Study area has been subject to extensive disturbance across almost two 
thirds of the Site. The archaeological remains across the portion of the Site outside of these areas of 
extensive disturbance are anticipated to be isolated and evide nce only transient land use such as 
isolated artifacts and rubbish pits, which may have been disturbed by continued development 
throughout the 21 st century. The potential archaeological remains are not considered to meet the 
threshold for significance.  
  
[[page 190]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  177  
Heritage Impacts 
 
The Proposal is located approximately 50m away from  a heritage item listed in Schedule 5 of RLEP 
2014, being ‘Cottage’ at 2 Richardson Place (Item 320). The heritage listed cottage at 2 Richardson Place 
is a rare remnant farmhouse which dates from the early period of subdivision and development in 
north Ryde, following the resumption of the Field of Mars Common. The DD notes that approval was 
granted to demolish the building doing the development of the Riverside Corporate Park in c2000, 
but the building was instead retained as part of that development.  
 
The view to the Site from the cottage is obscured by trees and the adjacent car park. The setting of the 
heritage item was completely and irreversible compromised by the construction of the Riverside 
Corporate Park and Honeywell commercial office building.  
 
As such, the Proposal would not impact on the heritage significance of the cottage. On this basis, the 
DD concludes that a Statement of Heritage Impact is not warranted.  
 
6.1.19.2 Mitigation Measures 
 
The DD recommends that should any excavation be required in areas of low historical archaeological 
potential as identified in the DD should proceed under an Unexpected Heritage Finds Procedure 
(UHFP). A heritage induction should also be provided to all construction personnel and the induction 
should include information about archaeology and obligations under the project approvals.  
 
The above recommendations are included in the Mitigation Measures Table provided at Appendix D. 
 
6.1.20 Social Impact   
 
A Social Impact Assessment (SIA) has been prepared by Willowtree Communications support of the 
Proposal and is provided at Appendix 27. The SIA has been developed with consideration of the Social 
Impact Assessment Guideline for State Significant Projects (2021a) (the SIA Guideline).  
 
The SIA has been informed by an analysis of quantitative data, strategic policy and qualitative data 
generated through a tailored  engagement process and ongoing refinements to the design of the 
Proposal and delivery provisions.  
 
Overall, the assessment highlights that the Proposal is likely to bring about significant positive social 
and economic benefits into the long term, through the delivery of digital infrastructure to meet 
increasing demand . The overall social impacts of the Proposal as described in the SIA  are likely to 
deliver benefits in relation to way of life, accessibility, surroundings, health, community, and culture. 
Based on the potential for residual impacts identified, the following mitigation measures are 
recommended: 
 
Construction Phase 
 
Recommendation 1: Before any construction work at the site, prepare and implement a Construction 
Management Plan (CMP) to: 
 
• Ensure the construction site is managed effectively and meets relevant environmental criteria 
and regulations, including detailed construction schedules, the phases, operating hours and 
communication strategies for adjoining properties in Julius Avenue and Richardson Place; and 
• Address the social impacts identified in this SIA and aim to preserve the amenity of the Site 
and surroundings, people's way of life, health and wellbeing and social connections, including 
traffic and parking, noise and vibration and air quality.   
 
  
[[page 191]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  178  
Recommendation 2 : Prepare and implement a Construction Traffic Management Plan (CTMP) to  
mitigate the impact on surrounding businesses to:  
 
• Manage the construction vehicles, including truck routes, size of vehicles, vehicle movement 
frequencies and plan for vehicle stands for loading and unloading  
• Manage construction deliveries so they occur outside of peak hours  
• Control vehicle entry points and turning areas where possible using a one-way system. Identify 
parking areas, delivery routes, and loading zones, away from the work area  
• Educate workers - traffic management in the induction for everyone  
• Communicate clearly with the community and neighbours, including provision and 
construction traffic routes and times.  
  
Recommendation 3: Implement the Tree Protection Management Plan (TPMP), which will need to be 
overseen by an official Project Arborist. The Project Arborist would manage tree protection and oversee 
any work conducted within the Tree Protection Zones (TPZs). This includes:  
 
• performing regular compliance monitoring and certification. Although several mitigation 
measures are already incorporated into the architectural and landscape design of the project, 
it is recommended that the following additional measures be implemented:  
o Replacement or offset of any trees and vegetation removed during site preparation and 
construction works, with a tree of like habit and indigenous to the LGA where possible, 
planted as near as practicable to the location of the removed tree, grown to maturity and 
replaced if the planting fails to survive and thrive.   
o New landscape areas to allow for supplementary native tree plantings to the boundaries 
of the site and the internal landscape areas. This also includes a diverse species mix of 
environmentally and culturally important species that reflect the surrounding a reas 
wetlands, grasslands, heathlands, scrub and dry sclerophyll forests. The tree line would 
help to screen the development and provide filtered views to the development. 
 
Operational Phase  
 
Recommendation 4 : Ensure that the operational noise and emissions are within the levels 
recommended by the assessing authority and guidelines (as identified in the previous section). The 
data centre design and management:  
 
• Implementation of sound-absorbing treatments where feasible in the designated galleries to 
minimise reverberation.  
• Appropriate sound attenuation measures integrated into the system to lower overall sound 
levels. 
• Final choices of equipment and design aimed at achieving the greatest possible reduction in 
sound levels.  
• Continuous monitoring and engagement with impacted neighbours, residents, and property 
owners as part of ongoing commitments to Stakeholders. 
 
Recommendation 5: An Operational Green Travel Plan (GTP) be implemented to manage the future 
impacts of operations related traffic and parking on surrounding road networks and communities. The 
key objectives of the GTP are to include:  
 
• Increased modal share for public transport, cycling and walking journeys for staff and visitors 
of the site;  
• Reduce private vehicle dependency as a means of access to the site; 
• Ensure adequate facilities are provided at the site to enable users to travel by sustainable 
transport modes; and  
• Raise awareness of, and actively encourage the use of, sustainable transport amongst users.  
  
[[page 192]]

Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  179  
Recommendation 5: The visual impact of the data centre ranges from low to high for the amenities of  
people within the visual catchment area. The bulk and scale of the data centre, along with its effect on  
the skyline, can have notable consequences. To benefit the local business and residential community, 
the following measures are recommended:  
 
• Enhance the architectural treatment of the façade to improve visual interest and aesthetics.  
• Increase planting and screening to soften the appearance of the data centre.  
• Engage with affected neighbours, residents, and property owners as part of ongoing 
commitments to Stakeholders.  
  
Recommendation 6: The development proposal is recommended to target implementing strategies  
to achieve Ecologically Sustainable Development (ESD) principles such as energy efficient design,  
procurement of renewable energy, water management and strategies to minimise embodied carbon  
in construction

6.1.21 Infrastructure Requirements and Utilities   
 
An Infrastructure Delivery, Management and Staging Plan has been prepared in support of the Proposal

6.1.21.1 Existing Supply 
 
Electricity 
 
The Site does not have any substations on the Site and does not appear to have any permanent 
electrical connections. Any connection to the Site appears to be minimal capacity low voltage 
connections. 
 
Telecommunications 
 
Before You Dig Australia (BYDA) plans indicate that the Site appears to have sufficient telco services in 
the nearby vicinity. 
 
Potable Water 
 
The Site has a frontage to a Sydney Water 200mm Ductile Iron Cement Lined (DICL) main located 
within the nature strip on the near side of Julius Avenue. The Site is unencumbered of utility water 
mains and requires no diversions. 
 
Sewerage 
 
The Site is located in the NSOOS 3 Catchment sub-system in the North Head wastewater system. 
 
Stormwater 
 
There is an existing stormwater system, owned by Council, serving the Site with mains located on Julius 
Avenue, which is not proposed to be utilised by the Proposal.

6.1.21.2 Infrastructure Requirements  
 
Electricity 
 
The new on-Site STSS of 132kV/33kV at N redundant arrangement with capacity of 180MVA serving the 
campus is sufficient to meet the electrical demand for the Proposal.  
 
Site 33kV MV cable reticulation from the new 132kV/33kV substation to the data centre building will be 
by means of underground cabling conduits and internal risers up to the transformer rooms. 
 
Backup Generation  
 
The data centre is a mission critical facility  and therefore requires back-up power generation. The Site 
is designed for a total of 72 x low voltage 3MW Data Centre Data Centre Continuous rated 400V LV 
generators to supply the data centre critical loads. An additional 1000kVA (1MVA) low voltage generator 
will be provided to supply the office/FOH and life safety loads (not shared with the Data Hall 
infrastructure). 
 
All data centre generators will be inside an acoustic treated enclosed plantroom with 1000 L day tank 
fed from bulk fuel tanks on basement floor. The office generator will be in an acoustically rated 
enclosure on the roof of the FOH building with its bulk tank in the basement. 
 
Telecommunications 
 
The Site is proposed to have two (2) sets of dual diversely routed underground pathways (4 off in total) 
for telecommunications cabling from street pits in Julius Avenue and Richardson Place to the two (2) 
Point of Entry (POE) telco rooms to serve the Proposal. 
 
A Conceptual Telecommunications Servicing Strategy is illustrated in the Infrastructure Delivery, 
Management and Staging Plan. 
 
Potable Water 
 
The Proposal will be supplied with potable water supply for internal domestic and industrial water 
supplied from Sydney Water’s 200mm potable network. 
 
Potable water will be supplied to the mechanical services industrial water tanks as a means of cross 
connection separation of potable and non -potable sources to feed all non -potable requirements to 
separate domestic potable water supplies. 
 
Potable mains water is the primary water source of the mechanical evaporative cooling systems serving 
the data halls heat rejection requirements. In addition, the potable water supply feeds water into the 
mechanical industrial water storage tanks prior to serving the mechanical systems. The mechanical 
industrial water system consists of 24hr stored water volume should Sydney Water’s water supply be 
under maintenance or failure for short periods. 
 
All tanks, mechanical systems and pipework within the Site will be owned, operated and maintained 
by the Proponent. 
 
[[page 194]]
ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT STATEMENT 
Julius Avenue Data Centre 
6-8 Julius Avenue, North Ryde  SSD-80018208 
 
 
 
P a g e  181  
The fire services connection will serve the aggregate fire hydrant and fire sprinkler demand 
simultaneously. Due to the effective building height and available pressure and flow data from Sydney 
Water, fire pumps and dedicated fire services storage tanks will be required to serve the development. 
Design of an efficient system will be subject to input and coordination with Fire & Rescue New South 
Wales (FRNSW) and Fire Engineering to provide data centre specific protection requirements 
performance solutions. 
 
Stormwater 
 
The Proposal’s stormwater design will be serviced by a series of drainage elements which will discharge 
the Site’s catchment. The matter of stormwater management is discussed in Section 6.1.13.2 of this EIS.  
 
Gas 
 
No gas supply is proposed to serve the Site, and the Site remains unencumbered by gas mains. 
 
6.1.21.3 Cooling System 
 
The SEARs prescribes the following additional assessment requirement in relation to cooling systems: 
 
Plant and equipment selection - The EIS must: 
• include a comprehensive options analysis of all commercially available cooling 
and back­ up power systems 
• provide a detailed overview of the proposed data hall cooling system including 
number and details of associated plant and equipment, and the energy and water 
demands of the proposed data hall cooling system 
• provide demonstration the development can  achieve a NABERS 3  stars energy 
rating or higher, including the Power Usage Effectiveness (PUE) calculations of plant 
and equipment required for the operation of a data centre. 
 
The Proposal seeks to provide a water-cooled chiller system for the data halls. TABLE 35 below outlines 
the estimated system loads for a water-cooled chiller system based on six-storey development with 12 
x 9.6MW data halls in total. 
 
TABLE 35. COOLING SYSTEM OVERVIEW
